CRICKET NOTES.

The ideal condition) which favoured the Whitsun- tide holidays were appreciated to the full by local cricketers who were thus enabled to bring off an interesting programme of matches without hindrance. The Warrington first eleven have every cause for satisfaction with their week-end's work for, after a somewhat monotonous succession of draws, two fine 'victories have at last been recorded. On Saturday Winnington Park visited the Arpley enclosure and did not give the locals much trouble. The outstand- ing feature of the match was the splendid bowling of the Rev. Horace Gray, who found the pitch just to hia liking, and it was chiefly owing to his deadly deliveries that the visitors were all out for 44. He captured six -wickets for '25 runs. J. Ellis was the only Winnington batsman to make anything like a good stand, his innings realizing 24.

On the Warrington side there were several useful scores, but noting particularly formidable. W Richardson batted smartly for his 28, and G Wood- cock, the promising young Botelerian, although per- haps not quite as confident as usual, put together a capital 21. Fisher, the home professional, also did good service, knocking up 20. When stumps were +drawn, the Warrington score stood at 92 for 9 wickets.

On Monday Warrington made the journey to Bootle with a team which included several members of the second contingent. It speaks well for the material which the club has at its disposal, that, chiefly owing to the efforts of the junior element, they were able to account for a team with such a high reputation as Bootle. It was, in fact, the first defeat which the Liverpool men have sustained this season, while on Saturday they made a creditable draw against Orton, compiling 221 for the loss of only three wickets. The excellent bowling of W. Bennett, backed up by the smart fielding of the youngsters, was in a great measure accountable for Monday's win for the Warringtonians. Bennett bowled with great effect, taking six wickets for 53 runs, and the home eleven's innings closed at 113. To this the visitors responded with 125. J Bradburn, a promising member of the second eleven who was given a trial, gave an exhilarating display, and headed the scores with 31. It was the partnership between Bradbarn and Woodcock (17) which made Warrington's position secure, but Bennett, the last man in, completed the victory 117 knocking up 17 ill his usual vigorous style.

'Warrington 2n& went with depleted ranks to Winnington Park and proved very deficient in attack. The home eleven declared their innings at 186 for seven wickets, W Lea distinguishing himself with a fine performance which realized GS. Seven Warring- ton wickets were down for 78 when play ceased, 11. H Lowe having the best individual record of 25.

The Warrington and District League tourney provided some interesting encounters, but the results were pretty much as expected. Warrington Conservatives, while retaining their unbeaten, certificate, found doughty opponents in Wilderspool who were beaten by only 9 runs. The scoring on both sides was far from brilliant, C Holt, who made 13 for the winners, being the most successful. Green and Holt put in some admirable bowling.

--- Whitecross Recreation were able to dispose of Orford by a margin of 4, with 6 wickets in hand. The villagers were all out for 23, P Ashcroft carrying out his bat with 10 to his credit. Whitecrosa put together 29 for four wickets, C F Maybury batting well for 12.

-- Cairo-street Unitarians were victorious in a very mediocre match with Sankey, the respective totals being 19 and 9. Needless to say, it was a bowlers' afternoon and none of the batsmen did anything worthy of note. The Rev Dr 2.1e110r was doing duty for the Unitarians and accounted for several wickets. On Monday Cairo-street suffered defeat at the hands of Orford, after a well-contested match. The Unitarians were out for 32, D Plinston batting in fine style for 19. Orford secured the victory by a majority of 5, P Ashcroft carrying off the batting honours with 11.

The following is the League table to Cate : P. W. L. D. Pts. Warrington Conservatives 1 ... 3 ... 0 1 ... 7 Whitecross Sankey 4 2 ... 1 ... 1 ... 5 Orford St Helena Cible 3 ... 2 ... 1 ... 0 ... 4 Cairo-street Rylands Walkers Wilderspool To-day's fixtures are : Wilderspool v Rylands Warrington Conservatives v Sankey Orford v St. Helens Cable Whitecross v Cairo-street.

There was a keen struggle between Warrington Secondary School and Padget°, the school achieving victory by I runs. Foulkes did excellent service with the bat, compiling 13.

Rylands' Recreation were easily superior to Leigh Anchor Cable Works who were beaten by 38. U Gough (13)and T Horrocks (El) deserve praise for their batting.

Lynim recorded a win at the expense of Liverpool Strollers, the totals reaching 77 and 57 respectively. T ißichardson distinguished himself with a fine not out nnings of 116, vvhile R Bell and E Rouse each made 13.

St. James' Bible class made an inglorious display against Latchford Park and at the close were 79 in arrears. The park just succeeded in making a century, the outstanding individual contributions being those of L Hainsworth (31), B Harding (25), and J Foulkes (12). There were no double figures on the Saints' score sheet.

Emmanuel Institute and Rylands' Recs, were closely matched, the honours resting with the Rees by five runs. Price and T A Knight each made 10 for their respective sides.

--- Emmanuel made up for Saturday'e defeat on Monday when they defeated Workingmen's Mission by the comfortable margin of r. 5. T A Knight was in top form with the willow and atca<sed 32.

-- 80ld-street NS'esleyans were beaten by the Working. nsen's Mission, but the scoring all round was very uninspiring, no double figures being recorded.

tiolborne-street Baptists were victorious over Cairo- street 2nd by 25 rune. For the winners E H Jones made 14, while E Whittaker compiled 13 for Cairo-street. T Woodhead captured five wickets for 8 runs and H Barnes three for 13.

To-day's fixtures in the Warrington Sunday School League : Stockton Heath v Workingmen's Mission. Orford St. John's v Brick-street.

The match at Winwick on Saturday ended in a draw. Winatanley Park scored 119 and Winwick were 69 for the loss of six wickets when time was called. The highest scores were 27 G. Humphries (visitors), 23 not out Dawson (Winwick), and 21 Floyd (Winwick). C. Cunninghont took Sour wickets for 44 runs.

THE CHURCH AND LABOUR.

Mr. Ramsay Macdonald, speaking on the Church and Labour, at Edinburgh, on Sunday night, said the Church and otganised Labour must co-operate. Must the Church be political? No, a thousand times no The Church to be political would have to abandon her claim to be enthroned in the hearts of all men. He brought no political message to them. He was sick of politic 3.

Crosileld's men played well and in fact there were only three of their men who lost but the handicap was above them. The captain of the Guardian team Was the first to win for his side and did so in fine style by beat- ing J Taylor by 15-5.

H Bromley (Crosfield's) gained three points for his side when he beat W Doolley by 15-12. After the seventh end the CrosBeld's man maintained the lead, and with some neat play won by the odd three. A Ashburner (Crobfleld's) was in good trim, and at the 18th end be beat W Caldwell by sit points. At the Bth end be had a lead of 10-2, but Caldwell got some good woods and ran his score to nine. A Robinson (Guardian) put up a good fight with H Littlewood, but eventually he lost by two points. The players were well matched, and the scoria were equal on no fewer

Hence and Taylor must have been inspired to • fine degree for be tlnished off Delaney (Whitecross) for 13-6 points. At one period he led by 12-2 but before he could get his one Delaney had added four chalks.

Oh! but, what a shock the Town Hall men got when their next man (Humphries) only scored a single point whilst Kynaston, the Whitecross representative, scored his 13. How short and how sweet it was will be seen from the fact that this feat was accomplished at the tenth end. Kynaston was responsible for some good bowling and had a couple in on four occasions. Chelton did not appear to be overcome by the fate of his pre- decesso: and after 13 ends he came oil the *Teen in triumph over Edwards (Whitecross) who had only scored six points.

This win gave the Town Hall supporters more con- fidence and Cotterill, the next man tor the Town Hall side was expected to come off well. But they had evidentlyinot considered Aleachin who, however, soon made his presence felt End after nine ends he disposed of Cotterill to the tune of 13-2. This was a dis- appointment for the Town Hall side but on the other hand the N.hitecross supporters showed their enthusiasm and B Williams took up the woods against Johnson with a cheer from his own side. However, after nine ends he had to put the woods down again with only six points to his credit. Johnson played a fine game and won by 13-6. The Town Hall team will enter the third round with high spirits and no doubt we shall Bee them very near the final.

There is no doubt about it that there are some very able trundlers in the Bewsey Ward, and when the Fair- field B team met Bewsey C the odds were well on the side of the Bewsey men. Councillor C J Holmes was the first man on the Fairfield side to try his skill, but his efforts proved pearly fruitless, and be lost to Morris by 1-13 after nine ends. Lennon brought the Bewsey teams' points 17 ahead of their opponents when he disposed of Bibby in the nest game by 13-8. Bibby appeared to have the best of the first portion of the game, and at the eighth end he led by 6-3 and gained another point in the next length, bringing his score to 7-3. This proved to be his last scare up to the 15th end, when his opponent led by 11-8. Two good woods and Lennon was home by five points.

Walker was the first man on the Fairfield side to lessen the lead and he did so by beating Woodey 13-9 At the ninth end he led by 10-3, but before he could get his three points Woody had brought his s3oro to nine. The next game was a very close one, for Wilkin- son (Bewsey) only won over McClue by 13-12. Thee was nothing much to choose in the players up to the finish and the score was equal on no fewer than eight occasions. There was a great amount of excitement when the players peeled with 12 each on the 21st end. but Wilkinson got a beauty and won by the odd 'un. Coyne kept up the style of his team when he disposed of Dunbobbin (Faiitiela) by 13-9. Dunbobbin showed better form up to the 12th end when he led by 7-6. Coyne then added two points but again on the next length his opponent led by one. At the 15th end the score was nine each and Coyne then scored beautifully and won by fuur points.

Broadhurst (Fairfield) did not come up to expecta- tions and left the green with only seven points to his credit, his opponent (Fluropton) having made 13 at the 16th end. Things looked very black for the Fairfield team but a win by Jolley over Dickens n (Bew say) of 13-8 aroused the enthusiasm of the spectators who Lad nearly lost interest but, however, these high spirits of the Fairfield supporters were damped, for the next two games were decided in favour of the Bewsey team, Clare beating Thomason 13-4 and Flynn disposing of Pharaoh by 13-2. F Scariabrick (Fairfield) was one of the four winners for his side and he came off the green triumphant over Carroll with a lead of six points. Brindle (Fairfield) was not so successful in his encounter with McMahon who scored 13 to his opponent 10 at the.lSth end. Brindle led for the secohd end but McMahon bowled well and peeled with ten at the 16th end. He then added his trio of points in the next two lengths and won by three points.

If all the Fairfield men had done as well as tbe last Iran, Houghton, the gime would have been decided altogether differently. He had as his opponent Councillor DicLensen and beat him at the 11th end by 13-4.

FLETCHER RUSSELL'S MEN AT GAIEACRE.

A party of about GO bowlers from Messrs. Fletcher, Russell and Co., Ltd., had a pleasant afternoon's enjoyment on Saturday last at the Bridge Inn Hotel, Gateacre. Leaving Warrington by the 1.27 train they arrived at Gateacre about 1.55 and a short walk amidst some lovely scenery brought them to the Bridge Inn, adjoining which is situated one of the finest bowling greens in that part of Lancashire. A main was well arranged and was quickly disposed of,the finalists being J Broadhurst, jun., and J Ellison the former winning somewhat easily by 13-4. Tea was partaken of in a large comfortable dining hall adjoining the bowling green and was admirably served by the host, J Wilson, Jun , and his staff of waiters.

===== 0002601_19120601_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
Swimming Notes.

The Warrington Swimming club will open the season in earnest on Thursday next when Woolton will be the visitors to the Legh-street baths. The following fixtures have been arranged : July 11 St Bridget's „ 16 St Helens away „ 23 Widnes away „ 26 Runcorn Aug. 15 Widnes „ 20 Woolton „ 29 Rin.corn Sept. 12 St Helens „ 19 St Bridget's Already the club has given evidence of very promising form in a friendly match at Runcorn and there is every indication that they NV 11 give a goad account of themselves as the season grows older. Runcorn were the first to press and Jolley in goal saved smartly on several occasions. However, the home team managed to score with a shot which gave the custodian no chance whatever. This reverse put Warrington more on their mettle, and some clever combination was shown, but Woods proved too alert for the visitors, and the teams changed ends with the home team leading by one goal to nil.

In the second half Warrington were aggressive, the home goal having some very narrow escapes. With the Runcorn backs playing a good defensive game, the Warrington forwards rose to the occasion, and J Graham's equalizing goal was the result of some smart work. On the restart Warringt ,n were still on the aggressive, but no further scoring resulted, a fast game ending in a draw of one goal each.

Prior to the match a squadron race was easily won by Warrington, who proved victorious by over half a length. The secretary (Mr. lace), 19, Causeway- avenue, will bc happy to receive entries for the works squadron championship up t) June 29th.

best. (Hear, hear.) Let 'them be patient and s, e how things developed. What were the needs of the hour? There was an en rmous diversity of opinion. Some would say we needed improvement in trade, others might suggest an equitable distribu- tion cf the population and they said that if they cculd get the people over the sea what a development there would be. (Laughter.) "Go outside and touch the sod and sovereigns would come up." (Laughter.) Some of them had a job to find a threepenny piece when they got across. (Hear hear.) Emigrati n was no policy of his. If they could only get the political parties of the country to forget their partisanship, Liberals to forget Liberalism, Tories to forget Toryism, Conservatives to forget Conservatism, and Socialists, to forget Socialism and if they would all join together and have three years—it would pay the nation to give them three years —and by their intelligerce let them settle on nothing but scriptural lime the land question of the country, then there would be room for everyone and plenty for all. Then again there were great social difficultie s to be overcome. He was a Socialist of Socialists but he did not catch his inspira- tion from men like Mann and Blatchford. His Socialism was born in Bethlehem and he caught the inspiration of his Socialism from a journeyman joiner who was one of the finest Socialists that ever lived and gave to the world the .finest Socialism ever seen. "Whatsoever ye would have others do to you, do ye likewise unto them." That would settle the terrible "sweating " question in two days. Let that come into practice and all the evils would be swept away. Everyone ought to have equal opportunities. " Workingmen be steady, and in Gcd's Name let the last thing you do be to strike.'' If only they would be steady they had got their face towards the rising sun and the broad daylight would soon follow. (Hear, hear.) He was no Socialist leveller, for so far as he had been able to read history there had always been rich and poor, and as for the future there always would be, but the gulf that divided the two was far too wide. (Hear, hear.) Another thing the nation needed was f r cur young people to have a just conception of all the elements which con- stituted manliness. He was not out of sympathy with the you, g having recreation, but we were paying to) high a price for it. (Hear, hear.) They should always keep first things first. \A hat were the ideas of manliness at the present moment? One or them was to be able to manipulate a cigarette (laughter)—and another was to mutilate the mother tongue All the principles that went to make a man were to be found in the New Testament. (Ffear,hear.) iWe needed powers by which we could deal with the great evils in tbe land, and first of all the drink traffic. (Hear, bear.) Even if he knew that , by so doing he would bring revolution to-morrow he would crush the monster under his heel. everything could be dealt with but the drink. The 'working men did not run the public houses. They Imust have back that Bill which was ruthlessly thrown ' out in 1908 and have it strengthened so that they could defend themselves. (Hear, hear.) Then again powers were needed to deal with the nefarious system of gambling They should have a law that gave the police the right to take a man at any time who was carrying on the gambling business If he found raffling at a church or chapel social he could take the minister. A greater spirit of enthusiasm was also needed in the churches. They did not want , demonstration so much as enthusiasm. There was a society in the country which had never been marked for demonstrations but they were indebted to that society for nearly everything they , had obtained—the Society of Friends, (Hear, hear). Every secial problem of the nation lay at the door of the church and would never be fully solved until the churth did ire duty. Give to the church the powers to deal with evil and give it young manhood and opportunities to wield every influence arid power and what would happen? Heaven was coming down and by the laws of its own gravitation it was drawing earth up. There was a midnight blackness changing int 3 grey. Clear the way and the redeemed England would be the pioneer to the reform of the whole world. (Applause). A comprehensive vote of thanks was moved by Rev, W. Cooper and seconded by Mr. J. Griffiths, with whom Dr. Dalton is staying.

few hints on egg-testing may be given. This may be done by holding each egg in front of an oblong hole (the size of an egg) which has been cut out of a piece of cardboard ; place a candle at the back of the open- ing, so that the contents of the egg may be seen. If th 3 eggs appear clear against the light, these may be discarded as unfertile. If on the other hand a dark patch appears, then you can be sure that such contain a chick. Da not throw clear eggs away as useless,for they are useful when hard-boiled as chicken food; indeed; some people use them for cooking purposes.

YOUNG COCKERELS. The early batches of young cockerels should now Lie weeded out, any that are unfit for keeping on should be separated and fattened up. Spring chickens fetch very good prices and there is an enormous demand for them at the present time. Get into touch with pro- prietors of large seaside hotels with your produce, they will give you the Lest prices, and this saves the trouble and expense of market tolls. A very good fattening food for early chickens is as follows. Purchase some seconds lice; boil this well and mix with crushed oats and skim milk to which has been added a piece of fat. This is a food which is not. only readily eaten, hut a food which places on the frame a good quality of nice white flesh. Ducx,;

_ Put down as many duck eggs now as possible. In about two months time there will be a big demand for these. About this period of the season there are always plenty of hens brooding. This will provide them with useful and profitable occupation. Do not put too many eggs under one hen; far better to have a few less and good broods, than a large percentage of breakages.

MR. HEIR HARDIE.

On Wednesday night Mr. Keir Hardie in the House of Ocrnmons advocated the nationalivtion by the Government of Mines, Railways, and Land, in order to better the wealth of the workiug classes. To nationalise every trade would, we fear, be impractic- able, and so far as the cl thing trade, on which the appearance and outward comfort of the people depend, the great ent.rprise shown by "SUITS LTD." in reducing the cost of high-class bespoke Tailoring for i the benefit of the working classes in particular, s certainly worthy of emulation in other trades. " Suits Ltd." have brought London's export tailoring to Warrington at prices' which are within the reach of everyone. The local address of "SUITS, LTD." is Jubilee Buildings, Horsemarket-etreet, 'Warrington, and the suit they produce for 20s. (and upwards) is wonderful value. Patterns are supplied free, and you can't do better than call at Jubilee Buildings, Horse- market-street, Warrington, and see "SUITS, LTD.'s " enticing materials. "SUITS, LTD.," Jubilee Build- ings, Horsernarket-street, Warrington.

Mr John Taylor, shipowner, of Sunderland, died under tragic circumstances on 01 ednesday morning. He was found with his throat cut among the graves at the Bishopwearmouth Cemetery. Bucklow Council on Wednesday considered a petition signed by 90 residents to discontinue Northenden Wakes on the ground of the nuisince caused by noisy musical instruments and refuse from the vans.

AUSTRALIA v. SOUTH AFRICA AT

VISIT OF THE PRIMITIVE METHODIST PRESIDENT. Sermon and Lecture at Hatton.

The villagers of Hatton were highly honoured on Thursday by a visit from Dr. Dalton, president of the Primitive Methodist Connexion. The rev, gentleman gave an ir.teresting address in the evening on "England's needs and how to meet them " and dealt with his subject in an able and suggestive manner. The afternoon service in the Church was a sur?rising success. Many friends had journeyed from Warring- ton and surrounding villages to meet the Piesident and to listen t ) his utterances. By the kindness of Mr. Peacock a motor was at the service of Dr. Dalton, a favour which was much appreciated. The text selected was Hosea ii. 14-15. The discourse will not be easily 'forgotten by those who were fortunate enough to hear it. It sparkled with wit and oziginality. To quote a familiar dictum "He preached a sermon that was chat acteristic, it was thoroughly Daltonian." From the text, the preacher showed that God's dealings with his people were mysterious. God allures His people iuto the wilderness—the last place to which they would choose to go. Next, His dealings with His people are always attended with a beautiful purpose. Even from the wilderness' hope would spring for those who put their ttust in God. Lastly, Providence always has a beneficial result in view. Even in the wilderness Israel was to sing. These ideas were delivered in a somewhat humorous vein, but with telling effect. The prayer was offered by Rev. S. Johns,n, of Widnes,and the singing was most hearty. While the visitors partook of tea, the circuit officials held the Quarterly Meeting. The arrangements for July, August and September were completed with dispatch. Despite some removals during recent weeks the membership was found to be upon the increase.

Mrs Johnson Wright and Mrs Cummins, who are over here upon a visit from New Zealand, availed themselves of this opportunity of meeting the presi- dent and several of their numerous friends.

Tea was afterwards partaken of and proved a most enjoyable feature of the day.

===== 0002601_19120601_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
ENGLAND'S NEEDS AND HOW TO MEET THEM.

. The importance of the subject of the doctor's lecture in the evening, " England's needs and how t meet them," was sufficient to ensure a large audience and the church was filled almost to overflowing. Mr. J. B. Starkey presided and the Rev. W. Cooper in introducing him said he was always willing to help in church work and was closely connected with them at Legh-street. The CHAIRMAN at the outset said the meeting that night was a revival meeting to try and bring Primitive Methodism to the front a3ain. In life nowadays they found so many religions folk that they did not know when they came across a really bad man. Evhrybody professed to be a Christian, but one found that some of them were only Christian men on the surface, and when a glance was taken into their lives they invariably found that they were not what they professed to be, but were lead- jog a double life. They wanted clean lives and a clean Church. (Hear, hear.) The cry now was that people were deserting the Churches. If they did not live a good life they could not be Christians, and it was the same in the Church life ; they must have men sincere. (Hear, hear.) Everyone now was crying out for rights ; not justice, but rights. It was all righs. Nelson had mid that England expects every man to do his duty," but now the idea seereed to be that every man expected England to do his duty for him. Let them see they were living up to what they preached. They would have to cultivate a brotherly spirit and take everyone in the churches. There was nothing gained by back biting. He trusted they would all try so to live that they would leave the world better than they found it. (Applause.) Dr. DALTON, who was warmly received, preceded his lecture by a series of humorous stories which he told in a clever and acceptable manner. The subject he had chosen was, he said, a great one. The magni- tude and importance of it increased as he rernemkered it was discussed by all members of the community. From the agricultural labour upwards, all discusied the all-impartant subject.. It was not only discussed here, but it was the subject of debate over the seas. Every man who got up to speak gave the im ,ression that he had found a panacea for all the evils under which we laboured. He was quite prepared to believe that the English public was as easy to be gulled as any public on the face of the earth. Yet he did not think we would open our intellectual throats and swallow anything a person liked ta tell us (Hear, hear.) It was the duty of all Inea and women to think the subject out for themselves for they wonld never lift society in the lump. They could cnly lift individuals as they got them to think for themselves. (Hear, hear). They should all make themselves familiar with what had marred the progress of the land to undei stand the subject properly. The question was one for the work- ing men of the country. All the landed proprietors, , great capitalists and great employers of labour had no rower to decide the destinies of this great empire. It , Was for the workingmen to guide the empire if they would not be gulled by " Jill of swill" if they would not be captured by a ride in a motor car and ' not be politically overwhelmed by the raising of a hat ' once in five years. The workingmen should have courage in their convictions and let those convictions be based on the knowledge of the history of the country. He was a political parson and he thought it was the sheerest cant to talk about severing religion i front politics. He thought they had left political matters far too long in the hands of unbelievers and if political affairs were hatched more in churches and less in public house bars, it would be far better for the country generally. If they could only get the working men of the country to think more for them- selves it would be a great deal better for all. He was not concerned about the unrest in the country at the present time for it was a struggle for the

===== 0002601_19120601_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
BLACK MINORCAS.

This is our second illustration of Black Minorcas, but the first one was that of a male only. The above picture depicts both male and female—both of very good exhibition type. I shall of course refrain from going into the merits and demerits of the above, seeing that I have already done so in a previous column. My sole reason for giving the above is to show what type should he bred for in this variety.

===== 0002601_19120601_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
TIIE BREEDING PENS.

Now is the time when breeding pens are broken up, aith the exception perhaps of a few pens of the lighter varieties, It is essential that you get rid of all old birds and make room for the young stock. Some people insist on keeping the old birds on. Now this is not profitable. If you want to keep a few of the best for another season alright ; but all the rest, as they cease to lay, should be fatted up and killed for market purposes. If you have had a good season in eggs and a good crop of chicks as well, you cannot expect much more from the stock birds. When fatted before dropping into moult they are much quicker off your hands; besides, they fetch far better prices. From 2s. 6d. to 3s. is readily given for fat young hens, and after all ; these are good prices considering the fact that they have done a season's work in the pens.

===== 0002601_19120601_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
TIIE RUES.

As soon as you have-cl;ar—e-ci the pens, give them a good top-dressing with lime. After this has been on for about two or three weeks the runs will be ready to receive the young stock. Do not ho,vever over- crowd the pens. This is one of the greatest evils in poultry keeping, especially amongst young stock, and even mote so in pens that have been previously occupied.

===== 0002601_19120601_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
NATURAL INCUBATION.

By the request of a reader I give the following few notes on the above subject. Nothing is more annoy- ing than having a valuable sitting of eggs spoiled by the hen not sitting her time out. It is not always con- venient to replace her in the space of half-an-hour. Be sure then that she is thoroughly broody, and never risk a broody pullet if an old hen can be procured. Observing these essentials you may proceed. Make the nest in the quietest corner possible. If on the ground flpor so much the better, providing always that it is not too damp. If the nest has to be made on a boarded floor then place in it first of all a good thick sod. After making firm and even, place a good thickness of soft hay and well sprinkle the whole with Keating's insect powder. Having thus far pro- ceeded you may introduce the hen to her new quarters. Do this at night and in doing so, place a few " pot " eggs under the hen. If the following morning she seems quite content and the false eggs warm, then you may risk the genuine articles These also should be placed under at night and when she is still on the nest. These simple precautions are very necessary to to avoid breakages. When a full sitting of eggs is placed in the nest and the hen allowed to go on of her own free will there is a danger that she may at first be somewhat clumsy and smash one or two.

===== 0002601_19120601_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
FEEDING- THE BROODY._

A sitting hen should never be fed oftener than once a day. This should be given about nine or ten o'clock in the forenoon, when the rest of the stock are away foraging and all is quiet It will be quite obvious that the daily meal should consist of a sub- stantial and good quality grain. Indian corn or maize is the very best to give, for it not only takes a long time to digest and waste away in the system, but it also supplies that necessary amount of internal fat so essential for providing the oil which gives heat to the hen's body. This feeding, you are aware, is to be avoided with laying st ck, but should be sought after in the management of the sitting hen. Very well, then, throw (lowa a good feed of c3rn, place a vessel of eh an drinking water close by and in a small box put a h3ndful of sharp flint grit. Don't forget the dust-bath. This should be composed of a shallow box full of dry sand and fine ashes, to which has been added a handful of powdered sulphur. The latter will destroy any existing vermin. Having thus far made preparations you may then release the hen. If not Inclined to leave the nest after a fair trial you should gently lift her from the nest and then prevent her getting back again until she has had a good meal. When you are satisfied that se has thus partaken, let her go back again but do not drive her on unless forced to do so. If the hen is thoroughly broody and' keen on 'you will have no difficulty in this respect. TESTING THE EGGS.

It is quite safe to test the fertility of the eggs after a week has elapsed. It is useless for the hen to cover unfertile eggs; the heat of her body can more profitably be given to the fertile ones; Therefore a

"RINGING THE CHANGES."

Elizabeth Reeves, a Birmingham woman, was on Monday, remanded at St. Helens on charges of ringing the changes and stealing 2s. and 4s. Gd. from trades- people in the Market Place. After the woman had been locked up her husband went to the police office and asked for some of the money that she had got. He was sent away, but returned shortly afterwards with a pair of boots under his arm, which, he said, be had stole°, and he was then locked up. He was now remanded along with his wife for inquiries.

THE EXAMINER.

SATURDAY JUNE 1, 1912.

THE SHIPOWNERS Ihe shipowners again AND LABOUR. stand in the way.

There was good reason to believe that the London strike would be brought to an end by the conference to be held under the auspices of the Board of Trade; but the prospect has been made blacker than before by the letter sent to Mr. SYDNEY BUXTON by Mr. J. A. POTTER, the Chairman of the London Shipowners' Dock Labour Committee. Mr. POTTER springs into fame for the first time; but the organisation with which his committee is associated has a far from ingratiating character in connection with labour disputes during the last twenty years. His letter to 'the President of the Board of Trade is finely characteristic of the views of a certain type of shipowner. In effect, it repudiates, with a certain scorn, the finely impartial and discerning report of Sir EDWARD CLARKE. The assertion is made that the report does not point to any breach of agreement on the part of the shipowners. It is only necessary to refer to the report itself to find out at once how far this statement is lacking in candour The pith of the matter is that the shipowners will not attend the meeting to which they have been invited by the Board of Trade. Their Dock Labour Committee, which seems to have power of action without calling them together, fails to see any good reason for attending such a conference. It is a futile thing to enter into agreements, it is said in effect. "The shipowners have no confidence and no assurance that any negotiations that now take place will have any greater reality, or be more permanent," than former ones And there the whole scheme for bringing about a settlement is brought to a standstill, so far, at least, as so powerful a body as the shipowners is concerned.

shall wa-t-cnic--;vit-1; eager interest the development of the shipowners' plans for dealing with the situation. They have not hitherto shown any special capacity in this direction. It will be remembered that what

'has now developed itself into an almost general labour unrest first took practical shape in a seamen's strike. The shipping Federation told the world that it was fully prepared for anything that might take place. No difficulty could present itself that it was not ready to deal with. There was, as will be ' readily remembered, an easy victory for the seamen, which immediately produced its effects in strikes involving other large indus- tries. A stone had been thrown into a pond, and it produced ever-widening circles of discontent. Certainly, other employers of labour have the best Ereasons for holding themselves free from the leadership of the shipowners, who for nearly thirty years past have, it is probable, spent far more money over fighting their men, and supplying excessively expensive "free labour," than would have contented the firemen and seamen twice over.

When the shipowners say that they will not meet the workmen's representatives in conference, they must have some plan of their own. What islit; and how can it possibly be carried out without just'such a meeting as the Board of Trade has proposed? Mr.HARRY GOSLING showed how well he understands the realities of the situation when he told the men the other day that if there was not a Conference at once there would have to :.be a Conference afterwards. This is indisputable. However 'long a war may be continued, there always has to be a meeting for the singing of the Treaty of Peace. Probably we shall be right in assum- ing that the shipowners expect to gain a victory by breaking the trade unions. Protection is being demanded for " free labour " ; and there will be taken, of course, every possible precaution for the prevention of breaches of the peace; but over how long a time do the shipowners propose to make it necessary for ships to be unloading and con- voys of goods to be driven from the docks between lines of policemen? The public will probably recall the fact that when "free labour" at the docks I was the established system, dock labourers were kept struggling outside the gates for tickets which gave them permission to work for a few hours.and which might fall into the hands of !the same men only at intervals of a few days. Can it be thought possible that this dreadful schema of casual emnloyment can be revived?

No local official pub - WARRINGTON'S lication contains more HEALTH. of interest to the public- spirited townsman than the annual report of the Medical Officer of Health. Like those which have preceded it, Dr. J. Coote Hibbert's latest review of sani- tary conditions of the borough provides a great deal ofmaterial for discussion and should stimulate the authorities to still more energetic action in the determination to ensure a fair chance of good health to all classes of the community. It is disquieting to learn that in 1911, there was a general rise in the death rates, but this was not confimed to Warrington, and can be entirely accounted for by the excessive mortality from diarrhea due to the specially hot and dry summer. Dr. Hibbert lays emphasis upon the fact th It there is no short cut to the attainment of permanently improved statistics, which can only be reached by the steady progress of sanitation in all its branches. The policy of the Health Committee has been directed along those lines and it is some satisfaction to learn that improvement in the housing conditions of the poor has been steadily, though slowly, taking place during recent years. Those who made the recent Health Week tour of slumdom, will readily endorse the doctors opinion that the clearing out of certain districts and re-housing of the displaced tenants in tenement dwellings are greatly needed. It is not creditable to a progressive borough that the medical officer should have to plead for a more thorough and efficient scavenging and cleansing of insanitary areas. The overlapping of committees is probably to some extent responsible for the present unsatisfactory state of affairs but it should be possible to come to an efficient working arrangement, ensuring those extra pre- cautions which common sense and a iegard for health and decency dictate.

Alike at the South "AN UTTER Hackney by-election, WEARINESS." which resulted in the

return of a Liberal Member, instead of Mr. Bottomley, latterly an Independent Member, in North-west Norfolk, and wherever Tory speakers are confronted with the task of convincing opponents, doubters, or waverers, instead of merely eliciting cheers from. enthusiastic supporters, the Opposition case is confined as closely as possible to the attempt to cast odium on the Insurance Act, while the questions of Home Rule, Disestablishment and even Tariff Reform are discreetly shelved. Even Mr. Bonar Law's final message to the Conservative candidate ignored Tariff Reform as though he admits it has become a dead letter. The political correspondents who report the meteorological conditions for the guidance of Tory workers have regretfully declared that on all these points the rural electors of Norfolk display "an utter weari- ness and stolid indifference."

It is only by attacking THE IRRESISTIBLE the Insurance Act that TEMPTATION. the Tories hope seriously to damage the Liberal position. In the very nature ot things, a great scheme of National Insurance must be full of complexity. It is easy,there- fore, to misunderstand and to misrepresent it. In the nature of things the average man, if he does not quite echo the 'sentiment of Shakespeare's Ancient Pistol—" Base is the knave that pays "—is disposed to regard the person or the Government who exacts pay- ment, no matter for what value, as an unpleasant acquaintance. The Opposition,' after vowing that no temptation towin votes

through the unpopularity that in the initial stages might be excited against a scheme of National Insurance should shake their resolution to support the principles of a great beneficent measure, have succumbed to temptation. Almost exactly a twelvemonth after Mr. F. E. Smith's magnanimous renun- ciation of every impulse to make Party capital out of the Insurance Bill, he has been declaiming with fiery rhetoric, at Henley, against the "unfairness to the agricultural labourer" of a measure to the principles of which he swore unfaltering fidelity. Nothing makes the Opposition more angry than a reminder of the cold water, and also the hot water, which they dashed upon the Govern- ment's Old Age Pension legislation. It will not be so very long before history will repeat itself in the case of the Insurance Act.

The conclusive answer THE ANSWER TO to the charge that the "SACRILEGE." Welsh Disestablish— ment Bill aims at "robbing God" is to be found in the tactics of the Opposition at by-elections. If the Welsh Disestablishment Bill really meant sacrilege, it would be easy to make the country ring with the charge. Mr. Lloyd George has thrown the venue of the con- troversy back from the nineteenth century, when the Irish Church was disestablished, to the Reformation. The argumentum ad hominem that the leaders in the cry against Disestablishment, as being sacrilege, , are members of a nobility founded on royal grants of Church lands, has had the effect which an argumentum ad horninem, if based on true premisses, always has. But the reference to the Reformation has a wide application. No one would contend that the employment oc. the endowments of a religious body which had ceased to be co-extensive with the nation on education and similar purposes was anything but desirable. The Welsh Disestablishment Bill proposes to do what, in the light of a more sensitive national conscience and of experience, every- one would have wished to see carried out wholesale at the Reformation.

Yesterday uas the tenth anniversary of the conclusion of peace in South Africa. * *

A goldfish and a Cocoanut were the two "presents from Lymm " which one visitor to the May festival was observed taking home on Thursday. * * *

Mr. Ramsay Macdonald, speaking on the Church and Labour,at Edinburgh, on Sunday night, said the Church and organised Labour 'must co-operate. Must the Church be political? No, a thousand times no! The Church to be political would have to abandon her claim to be enthroned in the hearts of men. He brought no political message to them. He was sick of politics. * * *

The New York Labour Bulletin for March last (N 0.50 reports that in December last the percentage of New York trade unionists who were unemployed for want of- work alone (omitting those unemployed owing to labour disputes or illness) was nearly one in three (319 per cent). This is the highest figure for any December since the record was first kept, even including the terrible year 1907, when it was 305 per cent. New York enjoys the height of "Protection," and the height of unemployment!

As far as the general death rate is con- cerned, Latchford now enjoys the distinction of being the healthiest ward in the town, with a rate of 127. It has, however, the high infantile death rate of 173 per 1,000, Howley having the worst record in this respect with 176. Howley, too, tops all the other wards with its general death rate of 206. White- cross comes out with a general death-rate of 143 and an infantile rate of 161. St. Austin's has the lowest death rate from the chief epidemic diseases-I'6—while St. John's has the highest with 3.8. • *

" The Whirlwind" visited us this week as will be seen from the article in another column. He came to complain of the smoke nuisance, and complained loudly and with a justification which we were forced to admit. One of his plaints was that the Health Committee's officers only found ten offending chimneys in the year 1910. A rather remarkable coincidence discloses itself in the medical officer's report for 1911, issued yesterday, for again the ntimber of offences is ten, though 233 chimneys underwent "formal observations." We shall be having the whirlwind coming in like a cyclone next week.

On Tuesday one of the most notable estates in North Wales was offered for sale at Llan- gollen. This is the beautifully situated man- sion and wooded grounds known as Brynty- silio, long famous as the secluded home of the distinguished Shakespearean actress Helen Faucit,and her husband,the late Sir Theodore Martin. It was here that Miss Faucit,one of the intellectuals of the early Victorian stage, spent her leisure after retiring from the glamour of the footlights,and here Sir Theo- dore Martin, the intimate friend of the Royal Family, wrote books and spent his old age. Bryntysilio (which is familiar to hundreds of Warrington excursionists) was one of the few country seats ever honoured with a visit by Queen Victoria during her long widowhood. Perched on a spur of the Berwyn Mountains, and commanding lovely views of the valley of the Dee, the spot is as attractive as it is interesting for its artistic and Royal associations.

WARRINGTON'S HEALTH.

GENERAL RISE IN THE DEATH RATES. -- Dr. Hibbert on the Housing Question. --- TENEMENT DWELLINGS SUGGESTED.

Some striking comments on the health conditions in the borough are contained in the annual report of the medical officer of healih (Dr. J. Coote Hibbert) which has just been presented to the Town Ccuncil. The following ate the chief points touched upon : It will be remembered that the general, zymotic and infantile death rates for the year 1910 were the lowest on record, these satisfactory rates being chiefly due to the absence of any Brecialontbleak of infectious disease and to the climatic conditions which were unfavourable to the occurrence cl diarrho,al disease. For the year under report I have unfortunately to record

===== 0002601_19120601_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
A GENERAL RISE IN THE DEATII RATES,

but, as a ill be teen in the following table, this rise has not been confined to Warrington alone : General Zymotio Infantile Death Death Death Rate. Rate. Rate.

Warrir gton I 1910 15.4 1.2 113 t 1911 16.9 28 117 7 f 1910 143 1•2 115 7 Great Towns 1911 16•4 2.29 140

During the year 1911 infections disrase was no more prevalent in the Borough than during 1910, hut the year was marked by a specially hot and dry summer which led to a large increase in the number of deaths from diarrlaral disease, esrecially among infants. The total number of deaths during 1911 exceeded those during 1910 by 76, while the number of deaths from diarrhrral disease during 1911 exceeded those during 1910 by 93. The increased death rates for 1911 can be thus entirely accounted for by the excessive !regality from diarthcra. These deaths from diarrkra occurred chiefly among children under two years of age, 83 of the total 141 deaths from this cause occurring among children under ens year, and 38 among these between one and two years of age. In this way the infautile, the zymctic (diarrkra being included among the zymotic diseases) and the general death-rates have been raised.

During recent years the main factors influencing the rise or fall of the Borough deOh rates have been the prevalence or otherwise of measles, whooping cough and diarrhoea. Fortunately during the year under report Loth measles and whooping cough were very little prevalent, but it is very evident that the prevalence of either of these diseases with that of diarrhoea would bring about a general death-rate very little below the high rate of several sears ago. There is

===== 0002601_19120601_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
NO SHORT CUT

to the attainment of Per .M.-ane-ntiiimproved statistics, which can only be reached by the steady progress of sanitation in all its branches. This too must be accompanied by greater care and a more acute sense of responsibility on the part of the people generally. Many cases of infectious disease would be prevented if parents took more trouble to ascertain the nature of indefinite attacks of illness among their children, especially when a sore throat or rash is present, and also if they were more careful as to the isolation of those infectious cases (patticulatly cases of measles and whooping cough) which are not removed to hospital. The common disregard for' measles is the chief cause of its frequent prevalence and of the resulting high death-rate from the disease, and the neglect of home cleanliness is one of the important causes of infantile diarrhwa which played so large a part in raising the year's death-rates. As regards general sanitary measures, it will he seen that a considerable number of insanitary dwell- ings have either been closed or put into a more sanitary condition during the year.

lIIIIIOVEMENT IN THE HOUSING CCIMITIONS of tEe poor has benn steadily, although slowly, taking place during recent years ; but the clearing out of certain specially bad districts and the re-housing of the displaced tenants in tenement dwellings such as have been so successful in Liverpool are greatly needed. Such tenement dwellings seem to be the only solution of the problem of obtaining low rented and sanitary dwellings for the very poor. If the death-rates of Warrington are to be improved much greater effort must be made as regards the

REMOVAL or REFUSE and excreta from the poorer districts and in connec- tion with the general scavenging of common coutts and yards. Indeed, in the hot months of the year, effort should be mainly concentrated on these quarters and the refuse and excreta removed thrice weekly. The provision in common courts of some covering f)r the ashbins or the adoption of some arrangement whereby the young children would be prevented from playing with the ash-bin refuse is very necessary. The expenditure of money in connection with the public health can be made in no direction which will give a better return as regards the increased healthi- ness of the borough than in the

MORE monopoir AND FREQUENT SCAVENGING and cleansing of these insanitary areas. Undoubtedly the poor have great difficulty in the storage of food there being rarely any,.specral accommodation for the purpose. The result is that odds and ends of food lie about the dwelling-rooms and in homes which are not kept in a cleanly condition, it thus becomes contami- nated and likely to cause diarrhcca.

TUBERCULOSIS. There have been, during the year, 152 deaths from Tuberculosis out of a total of 1,141 from all causes, or in other words one death out of about every 7 has been due to Tuberculosis in one or other of its forms. As special efforts have been made in this Borough during the year to deal with this disease in a more com- prehensive and vigorous manner, I propose giving a full account of the work carried out by the Health Department in this connection. (1) PREVENTIVE MEASURES. Tuberculous Milk.— It will be remembered that in my last report I referred somewhat fully to the marked extent to which the milk supplies of the Borough were infected with the bacilli of Tuberculosis. During the year of 1911 the Heath Com- mittee have continued the policy they adopted in the early part of the preceding year, and 79 samples of the milk supplied to the Borough from 72 dairy farms were submitted to Professor Delepine for bacteriological examination. Five of these samples were shown to be infected with the bacilli of tuberculosis. 6 9 per cent of the milk supplies of the Borough, which were examined during the year 1911, were thus found to be tuberculous as compared with 195 per cent:of those examined during the year 1910. The effect therefore of this energetic policy has been a definite reduction in the amount of tuberculous milk supplied to the Borough during the year under report. It is estimated that about SOO pints of tuberculous milk were distributed daily in the Borough during the year 1911 as compared with 2,600 pints during the preceding year. Although the danger from this source has been lessened, it is still present to a serious degree, and, as pointed out in the last report, the local improvement has been ti a large extent obtained at the expense of other districts, the tuberculous cows having been eliminated from the herds supplying the Borough with milk, and in many instances sent away to other dairy farms supplying towns which do not attempt to protect themselves by taking similar precautions. I must again emphasize the fact that if only the Authorities of the districts in which the dairy farms are situated were dealing with the matter at its source, by carry- ing out efficient inspection of the herds at the farms and eliminating those animals showing signs of disease, the greater part of this danger to the health not only of this Borough but of the country generally would have been avoided. INFECTION FROM PERSONS SUFFERING FROM PULMONARY TUBERCULOSIS. - One hundred and thirty- six persons suffering from ' pulmonary tuberculosis were notified to the Health Department during the year, either under the Public health (Tuberculosis) Regulations 1908, the Public Health (Tuberculosis in Hospital) Regulations 1911, or by Voluntary Notifica- tion. There were 106 sufferers from Pulmonary Tuberculosiswho died during the year,and if we multiply this figure by 4 (i.e., allowing 4 years as the average life ot a consumptive) we obtain the figure 424 as the probable number of consumptives who are living in the Borough at the present time. It is of course very evident that, unless adequate precautions are taken by these sufferers, they constitute a danger to the rest of the community, and efforts have been made by the Health Committee to minimise this danger. In order to:diminish the amount :of infection from this source, advanced cases of tuberculosis have been removed frcna their homes and admitted into

THE SANKEY SANATORIUM, and efforts have been especially made in this connec- tion at homes where the:proper isolation of the patient was not likely to obtain. The Sanatorium was opened for the treatment of consumptives in thp month of August, and the number of advanced cases admitted

I during the year was therefore only small (viz. 19), but the home conditions of these cases were very unsatis- factory and the go( d effect has been probably far greater than is suggested by the figures repre- senting tha number of sufferers dealt with. Four cases were also admived into • the but in the grounds of the Aikin-street Hospital during the summer months. 138 other consumptives, who resided at their homes, were visited at intervals by the Health Visitors and instruction given to them on matters of domestic hygiene and on the precautions necessary to prevent their being a source of danger to others.

_ BACTERIOLOGICAL L4I3OOATORY.—At this laboratory bacteriologichl examinations are made, free of charge, of the sputum of the patients who are suspected by Medical Practitioners in the Borough to be suffering from Pulmonary Tuberculosis. VISINFECTION, ETC.—Di,itifeCtaEtS, BrGIUM Curs and spitting bottles are supplied, free of charge, to any consumptive who is re•ident in the Borough. After the removal or death of a patient, mea,mes of dis- infection are carried out at the home.

(2) CURATIVE MEASURES.

A small pavilion (6 beds) at Sankey Sanatot ium is set aside for treatment of early cases of the disease with a view to their ore, and 12 patients were admitted during the yr. at-. In connection with the recer t INsUItANCE ACT

considerable stress-- is apparently to he laid on Sanatoria treatment for consumptives. This treatment I expect will include both early and advanced cases with the hope of curing:the early cases and temporarily improving or isolating the more advanced ones. As regards cure, 6e pal( rn is a very difficult ore. In the first place it is very rare to obtain knowledge of the case in its very early and curable stage ; hut even should one be fortunate enough to succeed in this, cure under the circumstances that are likely to obtain is I think very I roblemathal. The patient's stay in the Smatorium must of necessity be a brief , one, as unless these institutions are to be erected on a very large scale, the number of beds ava lable for such cases must be limited. After discharge fr m the Sanatorium, the pafei.t, in many instances, will have to return to his previous unsatisfactory surrounding 3, and those influences which b ought about the disease in the fist irStitce will again come into play arid aim( at certainly cause a recrudescer ce of the malady. For these reasons we should, I think, be

VERY CAREFUL AS TO THE EXPENDITURE OF LARGE SUMS of money on curative sanatoria, and especially so if measures are not to be taken at the same time to pre- vent as far as possible further spread of infection such as by dealing with advanced cases and by attempting to stamp out bovine. tuberculosis As regards the isolation of advanced cases, there %ill he considerable difficulty in inducing patients to leave their homes, even when these homes are more or less poverty-stricken and lacking in comfort, unless they feel they are going away with some hope of cure. It seems therefore to be desirable, if advanced cases are to be isolated, to have the isolation wards at the same institution as the curative wards. When the home conditions are fairly satisfactory, it is I think quite possible to educate the patient as to the mode of life he should live and the precautions he should take to prevent the spread of infection by giving him this instruction at his home and without admitting him into a sanatorium. A short stay at a sanatorium is of course of great value from this educational point of view, but if the case is too advanced for care and the home conditions do not require the removal of the patient to prevent the spread of infection, then with limited accomodation the beds should be reserved for possibly curable cases or for those needing isolation. The sanatorium at Sankey is undoubtedly serving a very useful purpose at the present time. The formation of an after-care con.mittee to help patients, who have been apparently cured or who are suffering from the early stage Ef the disease, to obtain, when discharged from the sanatorium, suit- able work under conditions as favourable as possible to their future well-being is very desirable. Dr. Hibbert records his indebtedness to Mr. Flood, the chief sanitary inspector, for his invaluable help and co-operation in the year's work. A table gives the vital rates for the different wards of the town. The highest birth-rate is in St. John's, 33-1 per 1,000, and the highest death-rate in Howley, 200 per 1,000. The infantile death-rate is also highest in Howley-176 per 1,000, against 74 in Town Hall Ward, 127 in St. John's, and 173 in Latchford. The lowest death-rate is 127 in Latchford ; Fairfield coming next with 13.7. THE SLUMS.

At the end of the year the Inspectors took a census of the population living in what may be called slum dwellings; i.e., dwellings which open into courts or which are so situated iti back and badly ventilated streets as to be under similar insanitary conditions. The vital statistics for the 12 months were then calculated for this population and are given below. The population dealt with in calculating the slum rates is a small one and therefore some reserve must be exercised when referring to the figures. The figures, however, certainly show • that there is a high mortality among those dwelling in this class of property.

On the queeii-on.of infantile-tinOit-iiii-ty the medical officer refers to the work of the health visitors ana the Mothers and Babies' Welcome. It is somewhat disapointing (he adds) to find that, in spite of the more energetic action of the Health Committee there is to be recorded a decided increase in the infantile mortality rate above that for the preceding year, the respective rates being 113 for 1910, and 147 for 1911. As previously pointed out in this report, this increase has been chiefly due to the increased number of deaths from Infantile Diarrhoea which have followed in the wake of the hot and dry summer. It must also be remembered that this increase in the infantile mortality rate has occurred notwithstanding that Measles and Whooping Cough, which have played a definite part in bringing about a high rate in some previous years, have been the cause of but few deaths during the year.

The 'Examiner, Saturday, June, 1 1912.

CHEAPER SOAP.

SIR J. T. BRUNNER AND CROSFIELD'S.

Sir John Brunner, presiding yesterday at the annual meeting, in Liverpool, of Brunner, Mond, and Company, said that their producton was now on a larger scale than it had ever been. The demand from every market was growing. A good deal of curiosity had been expressed about the effect on their business of their acquisition of an interest in Messrs. Crosfield's and Messrs. Gossages. It was his opinion that those two concerns would before very long make soap cheaper than anyone in the world. Be bad had some complaints about reticence of directors in regard to the amount of information they gave in their repirt, but they were always reticent, as it was well for the shareholders that they should keep their affairs to themselves. (Hear, hear). In a short time directors proposed to offer an issue of 28,322 preference shares, which would bring the number of such shares up to 150,000, which would mean a million and a half of preference Capital, against £2,287,000 of Ordinary. The price at which they proposed to issue the new shares was £l5, the market value being quoted now at 10 15-16. They were bound to offer these shares to their shareholders in exact proportion to the total amount of shares held by each, and the calculation they had made showed that only shareholders who held 75 shares would be entitled to any at all. The report was 'adopted, and dividends as re,com- mended declared, that on the ordinary shares amount- ing to 271 pr cent, for the year.

A woman named Mary Rose, who obtained a separa- tion order from her husband at Bolton alleged that among his acts of cruelty he used to sing to her a song entitled "To the workhouse you must go."

MACROOM CASTLE AND BRIDGE.

on an unwary stream of cyclists coming do on the grass track worn by foot passengers at the side of a road laid with new macadam on a Friday night and left unrolled till Tuesday morning. Of course it was "riding on the footpath." And lastly, but greatest boon of all, look at the improved surface of the roads, notwithstanding the little pits occasionally dug by the studded tyres of the cars. And with the main roads the side roads have shared in the improvement, perhaps through sympathy as the doctors say, and these secondary tracks are the cyclists' own at the week-end. Pity they are so little known to them. Look also at the dangerous corners constantly being cut away;at the narrow lanes constantly being widened ; the over-topping hedges that are being cut down. Who pays La. them ? As everyone knows it all comes out of motor taxes, to which we do not contribute a penny, either as tax—or ratepayer. Nay, it is the most recent as well as the most pleasant illusttation of the text, " Blessed are the meek, for they shall inherit the earth." A TOUR IN CORK AND KERRY.

If any one wants to escape the motors, I can recom- mend to him a charming way of doing it. Let him take the road or rail to Liverpool and, going down to Waterloo Dock, book a passage to Cork by the City of Cork Steam Packet Company's boat. Every Tuesday, Thursday and Saturday these boats sail across tha channel, ar.d the whole COO miles will coat him 255. The cycles are carried free, and that is worth consider- ing, when compared with the passage to Dublin. But he should write for a booklet to The City of Cork Steam Packet Company, Ltd., West Waterloo Dock, Liverpool, and obtain Ward and Lock's Shilling Guide Book "Killarney." It contains all the maps we needt d for a fortnight's holiday two years ago. The boat started half an hour late, at 8 o'clock, owing to delay in loading the cargo. Empty butter tuba, agricultural implements and machinery, plain and barbed wire, vans full of bananas, and 500 more sorts went with us, but no one could reasonably grumble either at the accommodation or catering for plasengers, of which there were about 70.

Llandudno and Rhyl were all aglow with light, and by the time Holyhead and the South Stack were passed we had turned in and slept as well as we could. About 10 next morning we were nearing Carnsore Point and we passed in between that and the Tuskar Rock with its fine lighthouse. Then the south coast appeared and receded and returned again, as the straight line traced by the boat gave it the oppor- tunity, and at last Roche Point, the entrance gate of Cork HarbJur gave us the first glimpse of this magni- ficent piece of water. Although in one place the harbour is 10 miles wide the cliffs at Roche Point are only half a mile apart, and the captain will often sound the syren to let his visitors hear the echoes as he passes the coast guard station. The course here to Penrose Quay at Cork is no less than 10 miles, so greatly does it curve, past IQueenstown, Ringaskiddy and Carrigaloe. As usual the Navy was well represented, the battleship Hooa, several destroyers, and two submarines were passed in the neighbourhood of Queenstown, and the whole journey up to the harbour, di ipping as it was with rain, was intensely interesting and the steep slopes wooded down to the water, crowded in many places with towns, or adorned with handsome residences, formed a succession of beautiful pictures. The city, soaked with limest-ne mud, proved itself a disagree- ably dirty place, and so we took the machines up to Capwell Station and left them there while we went to tea. The evening train took us 25 miles to Macroom as the downpour continued, and they carried our two cycles, male and female, free. Cloakroom charge for afternoon, Id. each. ;English companies please copy). Macroom Castle, built in the time of King John, was the home of the family of Penn,the founder of Pennsyl- vania. It is a dirty town, like all these in the south of Ireland, but the bridge makes a very unusual picture. The next morning, after a comfortable night at Williams' Hotel, we set out f ,r Kenmare. The whole district is simply a paradise for the English botanist. The royal fern clothes every ditch, the white water lilies cover every pond ; by leaning over the water I was able to gather a bunch that served as well as roses for a week at our lodgings. The valley of the river Lee is la'!, no means bog covered, but the waste of good land is painfully apparent, chiefly for want of young people to manage it. At ten miles we reached Inchigeelah and I had the distinction of photographing the National school children. Nearly every girl goes to school in bare feet, the boys seem to look after themselves or to be looked after better. For four miles we cycled along I.ough Allua, a very beautiful sheet of water, and then, at Bealanogeary some priests directed us ovar the Derrynasaggart Mountains t the Roughty Valley. The ir.ual route is straight on to Glengariff by the Keamaneigh Pass, and if you have not obtained rooms beforehand, Glengariff would be a better place to stay for the night,. Harvey's at the Post Office is all right. As for Kenmore I can honestly Eay that there was not another spot in the town we could have stayed at. The railway hotel or the Lansdowne Arms are above my pocket, but the Misses O'Sullivan, 22, Main-street, made oF ten days a most enjoyable holiday. Glengariff, or Killarney are more to the English

WESLEYAN CYCLING CLUB.

The advertised run for Saturday, May 25th, was to Mollington, so we set (IT along the main Chester road. After a pleasant spin we reached Dunham Hill, where Mrs. Blackburne, of the Cornhill Farm, made us an excellent tea. Turning to the left through Manley, we again stayed for the splendid vista over Chester, the Wirrall and the Denbighshire hills. From Moulds- worth the sts itchback-road through the Forest brought us to Hatchmere, the usual evening smoke being indulged in by the way. The rest of the homeward journey was via Crowton and Acton Bridge, and Stockton Heath was reached at 9.45, several of the machines bearing ferns or other spoil of the journey. Number present 19. Whit Monday and Eaton Hall are synonymous terms with the club. The usual route through Delamere Forest was chosen. and lunch was served at Mrs. Sutton's, Coronation Farm, near Deltsmere Station. The Iron Bridge was reached at 4.45, and after tea most of us enjoyed the walk through the wood, whilst one or two had the more domestic but useful pleasure of mending punctures. These last had the exciting duty of assisting at the rescue of a lady who had fallen into the river from a boat. The return through the Park and Chester was broken at Cornbill Farm for refre hments, and the 22 members present brought a most pleasant holiday to an end about 10.15. ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

"The Speckled Band."

Considerable interest has been taken in the first I production at Warrington of Sir Arthur Conan Doyle's latest dramatic creation 'The speckled band." Few heroes of fiction have captured the public imagination to the same extent as Sherlock Holmes and "The 1 speckled band " presents another exp!oit of the great detective. The play is every whit as enthralling as the original "Sherlock Holmes " drama, for not only is it bristling with incidents brimful of thrills and excitement, but it is notable for some powerful character studies. This is one of the few instances in which a story lcses nothing by its adaptation for stage purposes and the audience is kept on tenter hooks throughout. The evil genius of the piece is Dr. Grimesby Itylott, a retired Anglo-Indian surgeon, who has more of the fiend than the human being in his composition. The mysterious death of one of his step-daughters forms the subject of a Coroner's inquiry at the beginning of the play, but no-one can throw any light on the tragedy, which is put down to " natural causes." Later when the other step-daughter stands in imminent peril, Sherlock Holmes becomes interested in the case and is just in time to prevent the girl being murdered by the bite of a snake—the ingenious means by which the doctor had disposed of her sister in order to obtain posses- siou of her annuity. Mr. James C. Taylor makes himself a sinister, repellent figure as Dr. Grimesby Rylott and his violent fits of passion, alternating. with a wily,smooth-tongued persuasiveness, are admirably I simulated. It is altogether a powerful study which grips one by it realism arid intensity. Sherlock Holmes is cleverly impersonated by Mr. Herbert Bradford. His drawling, colourless mode of delivery in the second act tends to become monotous but when actually at work he is the personification of the keen, calmly confident sifter of crime. Miss Gladys Preston infuses much emotion and earnestness into her pre- sentment of Enid Stonor and Miss Dorothea Bretherton is distinctly successful as the adamant housekeeper,Mrs Staunton. Mr. C. V. Wallace as Rodgers,the tottering, fear-stricken old butler,makes a pathetic figure; Mr. George W. Barnes is very natural and amusing as Armitage, the village green-grocer and conscientious juryman ; Mr. Cecil Lloyd realises the popular con- ception of Dr. Watson ; Mr. Joseph Victor is sufficiently mysterious as Ali, the Indian servant, and Master Richard Jackson proves a general favourite as Billy, the bright and precocious page boy. The orchestra, under Mr. Boothroj d's baton, submits a very acceptable programme. NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME.

The management announce a novelty for next week in tbe shape of two dramatic shows each evening, at 7 and 9 respectively. The play to be presented is the romance" For love and the King" which has proved a great draw wherever it has been presented.

Itir. William Norman Blake, formerly a Wesleyan minister, is to be ordained a deacon of the Church of England on Sunday, and to be licensed to a curacy of St. Giles's, Pontefract, where he NM stationed for three years as second minister in the Pontefract circuit.

Insurance Agents and the Act. On Friday afternoon, 24th instant, a mass meeting of managers, superintendents, assistants and agents of all the life Assurance Companies and Societies was held in the I.L.P. room. Mr. E. Wynne Jones was voted to the chair. After a fine address on the variouis points affecting the interests of the agents by Mr Lewis, Manchester, a very spirited discussion followed, much dissatisfaction being declared at the duties of outside staffs not being defined. They con- tended their duties ought to be clearly defined as the payment was described as paltry for the work expected and if agents st md together a uniform rate of remuneration would be given, as some offices were offering double the amount of others. The following resolution was unanimously adopted :—" This meet- ing of Life Assurance Agents pledges itself to secure a fair adequate representation of agents on the local Health Committee by which the Act is to be administered, so that their interests 'right be safe- guarded," This being the first time in the history of local Insurance that officials and agents have met together, all hoped it would not be the last, whereby an exchange of opinions could be expressed.

TO HER SUFFERING

SISTERS.

A woman of great experience in female ailments o every sort gained great local celebrity for a herba mixture which was the means of saving numbers o women from great suffering. This mixture, now sold through chemists at 2s. 9d. (or 4s. Gd. for very large bottle), became known as Mrs. Lydia E. Pinkham Vegetable Compound, and its efficacy in soothing and permanently curing all the backaches, bearing down pains, irregularities, painful periods, and other women's ailments, has made it a boon to countless women. The accumulated results of Mrs. Pinkham's experience in all ailments of women is contained in a book, printed for private circulation, of which a copy will be send (to women only) in a plain envelope on application to The Lady Manager, c/o Lydia E. STOLE A CAVALRY HORSE.

WARRINGTON DETECTIVE'S SMART RUSE. At St. Helens, on Tuesday, George Themes Ripley, a well-dressed young man, was c' arged with stealing a Yeomanry horse, saddle, and bridle, valued at £3O. Frederick Marsden, 53, Brynn-street, groom, in the employ of Captain Mountford, B Squadron of the Lancashire Hussars, said that on Sumby night last he secured the outer doors of the s'avdes of the riding school at Pocket Noo' , in which were the horse, saddle, &c. The stable doors were left °pea by order of the veterinary surgeon. The horse was w( rth £25. and the saddle and bridle £5. They were the property of Colonel Lee Pilkington, of Norley Hall. Pr:soner had no right on the premises. Witness missed the horse, &c., on Monday morning. Henry Conner, of 34, Gee-street, Warrington, horse dealer, said that at 4.30 a.m. on Monday prisoner knocked him up and asked him to buy the horse he was riding. Ile said be had brought the horse at Lloyd's auction sale, Wrexham. Witness understood it was prisoner's property. He asked him why be was selling it so quickly, and he said he bought it for camp pur- poses. He asked him if he bad stolen it, and prisoner said," That is an insult ; I have a good mind to have you arrested." Prisoner was wearing a monocle, and when witness saw him put this in he said," I beg your pardon." Witness took the horse into the stable and arranged to find him a customer. Shortly afterwards Detective Holland, of the Warrington force, came into the yard and examined the horse. At the detective's request he yoked the horse, and they all three went for a drive, and finally he landed prisoner at the police office. During the drive prisoner said he would sell the saddle and bridle for 10s. NVitness had never seen prisoner before. Prisoner denied that he ever had a monocle in his possession. Detective Holland said he went to Connor's-yard at 5.15. on Monday morning. He pretended that he wanted to buy a horse, and had a trial run to the police station. Prisoner offered to sell the horse for £lO. Ile said that having completed his training with the Yeomanry he had no further use for it. When cautioned he replied `. I have an uncle named Dixon, a veterinary surgeon, who resides in Hall-street, St. Helens. He owes me some money, and I intended to take the horse and get my own tack. About 2 a.m. to-day I went into the stable for the purpose of having a sleep, and as there was no watchman there I took the horse, saddle and bridle, and rode it to Warrington, and offered to sell it to Mr Connor and yourself." Prisoner said h. was hard up. He had some drink, and went to stay the night in the stable. He had done this before, but there had always been a watchman. and he was tempted and took the horse. He pleaded guilty. Lnief Constable Ellerington said that on January 9, 1911, Ripley was indicted at Leeds for stealing a horse, and was bound over for two years. He was handed over from Leeds to the St. Helens Probation Officer. The Bench decided to deal only with the offence of stealing the saddle and bridle, and sent Ripley to gaol for four months' hard labour.

----- NEGLECT OF CHlLDREN.—noloert Pemberton, 27, 'Winwick-road was charged at the instance of the N.S.P.C.C. with neglecting his fur children. Mr. Browne conducted the case on behalf of the society.
