On the 21th inst. there was not a scrap of fuod in the house, and the society's inspectcr had given relief. They had had no food in the house for a fortnight, except that which had been given to them. In concluding her evidence, the wife described her husband as a man who wanted to work one month and play eleven in a year.—Prisoner said be had tried to get work, and had looked after them as well aa he could.He remarked that he had work to go to that morning.—The Chief Constable: It is astonishing that in these cases it is invariably the plea that the prisoner has work to go to the morning he is brought up.—Prisoner : You can make inquiries and see if I am not stating the truth.—Pemberton was sent to prison for a month.

RECTOR'S FUTILE CANVASS.

During the past week the rector of the parish of Glan Conway, which adjoins Colwyn Bay and Llandudno Junction, walked a distance of 25 miles, visiting the farmers of his parish with a view of procuring their signatures to a petition against the disestablishment and disendowment of the Church in Wales. The rector has bt en quite candid in stating that be did not obtain a single signature from the farming fraternity during the whole of his long walk.

Diocesan Sunday School Institute. The Training of Sunday School Teachers. Recent years have witnessed an increasing interest in Sunday school reform and great emphasis has been laid upon the need for the better equipment of those engaged in this in portant work. Among the efforts I made in many parts of the country one of the most successful has been the" summer school week" held in different training colleges, (including Warrington), where for a week Sunday school teachers engage in learning something of the best educational methods and in prsctical work along modern lines. Already two such " weeks" have been promoted with much success by the Liverpool Diocesan Sunday School Institute, and in the first week of July this year they base arranged by the kind permission of the principal and governors of Chester Training College, to hold their third summer school for clergy and Sunday school superintendents and teachers. On this occasion the accommodation, which is necessarily restricted, is available for teachers in Sunday schools of the Liver- pool and Chester dioceses. Lady students will occupy rooms in the College, while arrangements are being made for male students in the city. Students assemble on Monday, July Ist, leaving on Saturday, the 6th. The mornings and evenings of each day will be occupied with lectures, demonstrations, criticism lesson=, practical work, &c., all the instructions being graded to meet the requirements of teachers of different departments. The afternoons are left free for recrea- tion and excursions to places of interest in the neigh- bourhood of Chester. In addition to the college staff—who will each morning take charge of the demonstration and criticism lessons—the lecturers will include the Rev. Canon Morley Stevenson, M.A., Rev. E. V. E. Bryan, M.A. (Sunday school organiser Canterbury diocese), Rev M Linton Smith, M.A., Mr. H Herbert Fells, M.A. (London), Miss Phyllis Dent (London), and Miss Ethel G. Drillield (hon. adviser to kindergarten Sunday schools for the Liverpool diocese).

Christian Endeavour Convention.

At Glasgow, on Monday, tbe twenty-second annual British Christian Endeavour Convention was opened. There were several hundred delegates. Mr C J Youngs, Cardiff,-presided at the morning session, when addresses were given ty mission workers in the Far East and Africa. They spoke hopefully of the progress of Christianity in those lands. The annual meeting of the Union was held in the afternoon.

Bishop on the Need of the Church. The Whit-Sunday services at St Ann's Church were of special interest, in view of the visit of the Bishop of Liverpool who preached at evensong. In the course of an impres,ive sermon his lordship said the one great need of the Church to-day was spiritual power. During the last halr century they had seen the land covered with new churches ; churches bad bean restored ; vast sums of money were collected for Christian work at home and abroad ; settlements were planted in all the great cities ; and a whole army of missionaries was going forward to convert the heathen. A widespread interest had also grown up in Christian work, and religions matters were freely talked about in tram, train, and club. had there, however, been a corresponding growth of spiritual progress, and were Christians more straightforward, more Christ-like, and moreielf-sacrificing than those of half a century ago ? Was the Church to-day getting hold of the masses at home, and was it leavening the great movements which were rising up on all sides, and which they could no more stop than the incoming of the tide ? Was it not a fact and one to be most deeply deplored, that on all sides Christian ministers were mourning over a decrease in the number of mern'-ers of their churches, and that the attendance at public worship was lees than in former times? Was the Bible read or the Lord's Day observed as it used to be ? There was at the present day a cry for more spiritual power, but in order to receive it they needed faith, a submissive will, love and prayer. A new organ has recently been placed in the church and will be dedicated on June 26th Mr. Andrew Carnegie has given £125 towards the cost and of the balance of £25 to be provided by the Church, between £l5 and £2O remains to be subscribed.

Wanted : An Earthquake !

Nothing short of a terrible earthquake or violent explosion (writes the Rev A M Mitchell in the Burton- wood Parish Magazine), will arouse hundreds of parishes to a sense of Christian duty. Pockets are full, but Church alms chests are empty. Modern sinners are chiefly financial sinners—those who gaily ignore the weekly contribution to charitable and religious works. Thf se have eyes, but see not the duty and privilege of almsgiving. The beatitude of the nominal Christian reads : " Ms more blessed to keep what we have and to scramble for more, than to part with a proportion and do with less ! ' For bow niany religious tragedies of which the world bears nothing, knows nothing, are nominal Christians responsible ? To starve the Church and slay the parson is not an infrequent record of ecclesiastical parishes, some luxuriously wealthy, others shockingly destitute. Here is a common case calculated to make us "think furiously." A clergyman having for many years spent freely of his own in his parish, finally found

Christ would lead them to a church unity. The average attendance at the meetings during the past year Lai been somewhat less. The registered membership reached over 300 at the close of the year. The prize books obtained were 338, over 190 less than last year, but this as partly due to the fact that many members offered to forego their prize books until the time of finaneal strain was over. Yet there was a falling off in membership and average attendance. The open meetings were not as large as formerly. During the past nineteen years the number of prize books received was 14,367. The National P.S.A. Sunday had become a notable day and it was fittingly observed at Wycliffe. The movement was sending forth its branches in different parts of the world. In 1875 John Black ham founded the first society in Ebenezer Congregational Church, \Vest Bromwich. The brotherhood row numbered in Great Bri,ain 600,000 members, with 40 district and county federations and 2,000 societies. The report concluded with a reference to the impending retirement of the president Rev John Yonge) who had filled that office since the formation of the society. The name and work of Mr Yonge would live long and would be an inspiration to those who were permitted to labour at Wycliffe. They wished Mr and Mrs Yonge many happy years and hoped they would richly enjoy health and strength to the end of their days. The members were living in great hopes that they would have the pleasure of their presence from time to time. The Treasurer (Mr H Osborne) presented the financial statement.

The following officers were elected :—President (pro tern), Mr B Farrar ; vice-presidents, Messrs B Farrar and W Beswick ; treasurer, Mr II Osborne ; assistant treasurer, Mr R Bond; general secretary, Mr B Farrar; assistant general secretary, Mr W Farrar ; registration secretary, Mr W Rutter ; absentee secretary, Mr J Himsworth ; benevolent fund secretary and treasurer, Mr Joseph Owen ; markers, Messrs W and H Farrar,J J and E Hackett, stewards, Messrs D W McEacbern, H Osborne and W Palmer ; welcome stewards, Messrs A Healey and G Smiley : general committee, Messrs W H Parton, C Cotton, J Cotton, A LittlE wood, J Gresty, J H Archer, S Massey, H Massey, A Thomas, ft Daniels, W Roberts, J Biiley, H Blane, T Hallows, J A Bradley, E Ball, F Mullery, T Attwell.

St. John's Presbyterian Church. On Sunday evening the Rev. W. Reid preached on " Joel and his Message." He said the prophecy of Joel arose out of the devastation of the land by locusts repeated apparently for several successive years, and at a date which appears to place it among the last, if not the last, of the so-called minor prophets, The feartul ravages made by the locust were to Joel the text of the theme, which was a call to a quickened sense of God's presence and the results that would flow therefrom. Among other things this fuller baptism of the Spirit of God meant loftier idesls among both young men and old, and a prophesying pew as w ell as pulpit, while multitudes were to be gathered into the Valley of Decision. In all this, however,' and seen as the other side of opportunity and privilege, was judgment, the inevitable accompaniment of all life. To become that which received and transmitted the Spirits'rays of light, and the fuel upon which His mystic fire must burn was the call addressed by the old prophet to his day, and ' with even greater intensity by the Spirit of Pentecost to this day. GENERAL BOOTH'S LOST SIGHT.

The following bulletin was issued at six o'clock on Thursday evening :—" It is now evident that there is little hope of preserving General Booth's sight. Ills general health remains good. The pulse and tempera- ture are normal, and although he is deeply disappointed his spirits are fairly well maintained." General Booth has now lost the sight of both eyes. Some ycars ago he became blind in one eye as the result of dust getting into it on one of his motor tours. Latterly he had btcome almost blind in the ether eye, and the operation performed last week for the removal of a cataract will, it is to be feared, proved unavailing. General Booth is 83 years of age. Since the operation the King, the Duke of Argyll (on behalf of 11.11.11. the Princess Louise), the Earl of Aberdeen, the Duke of Portland, Lord Rothschild, the Marquis of Lincolnshire, Lord Northcliffe, Lord Armitstead, Lord Iveagh, Sir Abe Bailey, and many others have sent letters and telegrams of sympathy and inquiry.

Sir William and Lady Lever Lave issued invitations to a dinner at Thornton Manor on Saturday next to meet Miss Gladys liavenscroft, the lady golf champion. The body of a man, believ3d to be that of David Myers, of Yeadon, brother of Hubert Myers, the ex-Yorkshire Coutty cricketer, was found decapitated on the Midland Railway near Apperley Bridge on Thurs- day.

TREASURE- TROVE.

BY MRS. LOVETT CAMERON, iuthor-of "Juliet's Guardian," "Deceivers Evcr," "Jack's Secret," "A Sister's Sin," "In a Grass Country," "A Soul Astray," "A Passing Fancy," &c.

The first time I saw her, she was sitting on a heap of stones by the roadside, crying bitterly. 1 was just coming out of my own gate at Torsbury, meaning to take a little trot down to the village to see how one or two of my old pensioners Were getting on, when my eye was caught by a little huddled-up female figure clad in navy blue serge, wiith her head bowed down so low that it almost rested tipn her hunched-up knees. The knot of back hair beneath the brim of her black sailor hat was 1,, and soft, but there was nest to nothing to ee xe n of her face, because it was hidden in le-r n,,:oved hands. There were no rings on her hands, but they were white and well- shaped—with slender, tapering fingers, and oval, Pink nails—the hands of a lady. And she was crying. Not loudly or vulgarly, but just quietly and heart-brokenly, with little soft-breathed sobs that shook her body tremulously, as the leaves of a tree are shaken by the summer breeze.

I suppose I am a very soft-hearted old woman, for I never can endure to see a young creature cry. It goes to my heart straight • tears, I always say, are the portion of the old—they are our solace and relief in our sorrows; but tilt, young should have no sorrows.

I stopped, of course, in front of her—she did not move; then I spoke to her. "Is anything the matter?" She lifted two bewilderingly beautiful blue eyes, and looked at me solemnly. "Everything is the matter," she said.

She was very pretty, and very young—about twenty or twenty-one, I suppose. "You seem in great trouble. Can I not help you ?" I asked, pityingly. She shook her head. I noticed that her pretty face was not in the least disfigured by her tears— red nose and swollen eyelids were not to her apparently, the consequence of heart-broken sobs. "I don't suppose anybody can help me," she answered, gloomily, after a little silence. "I —l— have lost my all I" "My poor child!" My sympathetic thoughts flew to a family holocaust father, mother, brothers, sisters—had death bereft her of them all ? Yet she was not in mourning. Before I could question her further, she explained. "I have lost my purse, my railway ticket, my box--"

"Oh, is that all !" I cried, and the sense of relief was so great that I actually laughed merrily. "Come, cheer up! That is not so bad." "Not so bad !" she repeated. "I don't know what you call 'not so bad.' It's about as bad as anything can be—considering that I have not another penny in the whole world I" I was sobered at once, but I became practical at Vie same time.

"Come, get up, at any rate; to go on sitting by the roadside crying won't make things any better, my dear. Tell me your story and your name, and I will see if I can help you." She rose to her feet, and I noticed that she had with her a good-sized handbag. Her figure was slender and graceful, and her clothes were well- made, and fitted her nicely. She was a pretty girl altogether. She soon told me her story—a poor, sordid little story of poverty and toil. She was quite alone in the world, she said, and had worked for her living for some years past first as a type- writer in an office and latterly as a companion to an old lady. The old lady had lately gone to live with a married daughter and had dismissed her. Then she had answered an advertisement from an old gentleman, who never moved out of an invalid chair, and who wanted a resident Ildy as reader and secretary. He lived in York- shire, and would engage nobody without seeing them first. He sent her money for a third-class ticket from London, and told her to bring her luggage with her in case lie liked her, and to come off and see him—she was to go on approval, in fact. She spent almost the last shillings she possessed in getting herself a suitable outfit, and started with three-and-six in her pocket, and a fixed determina- tion to please or to perish. When she got to York, where she was to change on to a branch line, she was met by a messenger with a letter to tell her not to come any further as the invalid gentle- man had died suddenly in the night, and so she was not wanted. Money for a return ticket was given to her, and as there was nothing else to be done she got into the first train going back to town. Half-way, just as the train was slowing into this station, she put her hand into the outside pocket of her jacket, and found that her purse was gone. Some rough-looking people had got out at the last station, and she supposed one of them must have stolen it. Penniless and ticketless, she was afraid to go on, so she got out at our little country station. She did not even know what had become of her box, for in her dismay and confusion at York she had forgotten it altogether. She had absolutely nothing with her but the handbag ohs carried, which contained, she said, a few toilet necessaries.

Well, I am an old woman and a widow, and if I choose to play the good Samaritan there is nobody to prevent my doing so; so I took Monica Smith—that was her name—home with me.

We telegraphed all over the country for her box, but although the railway officials kept on assuring us that it was bound to be found in time, it never turned up, nor have we ever set eyes on it from that day to this. Monica meanwhile pored over the advertisement columns of the papers used by employers of poor ladies, and she wrote a great many letters, but nothing came of them ; either she received no answers or else the replies were unsatisfactory. Then, at my advice, she inserted an advertisement herself, but nothing came of that either.

Two or three days went by in this fashion, and during the course of them, somehow, I grew fond of Monica Smith. She was so sweet and gentle and pretty, it gladdened my eyes to see her come into the room. My house used to be so cheerful and full of life—long ago—and now it is so silent and empty, and the sight of her slight figure moving about the big rooms, or the sound of her footsteps flying up and down the wide oak staircase made my heart quicken and beat in a queer, unexpected fashion. I found out all about her first—l was very sly, I considered, and very clever. Her father had been a clergyman—he had been vicar of Marting, in Somersetshire, ten years ago. I looked up an old clergy list and tried to find the place and the name, but there were so many clergymen named Smith ; and then she said I had mistaken her—he was not the vicar, only the curate, so, of course, his name wouldn't be down. However, that was a mere trifle. What seemed so sad about Monica was that she had not only apparently no relations, but also no friends. She was very well educated; she had been at a high school in Gloucester, and an aunt, now dead, had paid for her schooling. "Her name was Smith, too ? " "Yes, Miss Mary Smith.' "A sister of your rattier's, I suppose ?" I wrote to that school in Gloucester. It was there all right. But the lady superintendent was a new one ; the one who had taught Monica was dead. This lady wrote most civilly and said she had looked through the old books of the establish- ment, but there were at least forty-five girls of the name of Smith who had been educated at the school during the past ten years, and three Monicas amongst them. This seemed conclusive, for she must be one of the three, and, besides, I was ashamed to ask for further information, so I let the matter drop. Moreover, I felt I had done enough for prudence' sake. It was time to let my inclinations have their way.

Full of excitement I came quite with a bounce into the library, where Monica was wont to spend her mornings answering advertisements. I found her, not at the writing-table, but standing in front of a large three-quarter portrait that rests upon an easel of Jack, my Jack, my only son, who was out in:South Africa in command of the Middlesbire Yeomanry. Monica gave a 3mnp as I came in and turned crimson. I blamed myself for my impetuosity, it is really quite unseemly in an old woman.

"Oh! " she gasped. "I --I beg your pardon, Mrs. Somerville."

"My dear, I startled you. Why should you beg my pardon ? What have you been doing?" She looked almost guilty for a moment—l really fear Monica's nerves are all to pieces with her troubles—then she answered:

"Oh, I was going out to post my letters." "Don't post them ; put them in the fire," I said. She opened her blue eve at me in amazement. I ;aughect. "My dear child, 1 have something very important to say to you. Leave the letter;, put on your hat, and come out into the garden,"

But when we were in the garden it was some minutes before I could speak. When I began I did not say it at all as.l.had melt to say it.

"Monic;," I began, falteringly, "I saw you were looking at my son's portrait.' She turned on me a startled glance. "Oh," I continued, "I am not going to scold you for that. Jack is worth looking at, even in a photograph. Monica, he is my anly child, and there he is out in that terrible war, and at any moment I may hear that he has been killed," I suppose I speak my thoughts too plainly and brusquely, for that poor child shuddered as if I had cut her With a whip. I passed my arm affectionately through hers. "My dear child, I only say that so that you may see what a lonely eld woman I am. Once I had them all—a loving husband, two sweet girls, a younger son. All now have been taken from me, all are dead. Only Jack is left, and he has been a constant sorrow to me ever since he came to man's estate." "011, Mrs. Somerville!"

"Ali, you can't understand my saying that. But I am going to tell you about him. My dearest son has caused me grievous misery. Ire married without my knowledge or consent in a way that I would rather have died than that he should have married—"

"Why ?" she just breathed. "Because she was of that class of woman who make false wives and bad mothers—she was an actress." _

A little silence. I snppose I frightened the poor girl, I spoke so sternly ; but that is a subject on which I feel very hard and bitter. "She might have been a good girl," she rnurniured.

"My dear, you are sweet and charitable, and you are also a little lady to your finger tips, and so-you don't know what those women are. lam older, I know. I am not speaking of great actresses like our Ellen Terry or our Mrs. Kendal. She was nothing like that. She was just one of the scum—a member of a third-rate country com- pany, playing in little parts—horribly badly, I daresay—and of course her origin must have been of the lowest. I never inquired. I never saw her, I am thankful to say, but for two years my son was a stranger to use on account of her, for I would not receive her; I preferred to quarrel with him. Then he had to go to Africa. He came to say good-bye to me, but he was angry and cold, and we parted in estrangement Then after a moment's gloomy retrospection I resumed more brightly: "But better times are dawning for me. Thank Heaven that creature, his wife, is dead! You are shocked at me, Monica? It's dreadful to you, I daresay. to hear an old woman rejoicing over the death of a fellow-creature • but I was glad, and I am glad. What is the use optetending that lam sorry? I may be a wicked woman, but lam not a hypocrite. There was a fire. It was at a country town theatre, at Carlisle. Several of these wretched winnen perished in the flames, and Miss Dale—that I believe was her stage name—was mentioned in the paper amongst the victims of the catastrophe. I sent the paper out to my son." "Was he sorry ? " "Oh, yes, I suppose lie was at the time. The woman had been his wife, you see; but he must have pretty well got over the shock of it by now, and after all it was a very mistaken marriage, and he has had plenty to do. This war does not leave a man much time for fretting over private griefs ; besides, it is a year ago now, and his letters have been quite like his old self lately. His heart, which that creature had stolen, has come back to his mother now."

There was a little silence whilst we paced the terrace from end to end together. It was a lovely spring morning. The woods were all bursting into tender greenery, and the distant glimpse of the sea between the shoulders of the hills was like a silver shield glittering far away in the April sun. As Monica said nothing 1 began again. "And I have told you all this, my dear child, because I want you to do a great kindness to a very lonely, and not very happy, old woman. I want you to live with me altogether--" "Oh, Mrs. Somerville I As—as your secretary, your companion ?" she faltered. "Not at all. As my daughter, my child,- my con- solation. I have fallen in love with you, Monica, and I can't live without you. Do you refuse my request ?" How could she refuse it ? She was poor and desolate, and I was her only friend. The tears streamed down her face as she kissed my hand, and she murmured, a little brokenly : "Oh, how good God is !" I drew her to my breast and kissed her tears away. So Monica Smith lived with me, and I grew to love her very dearly. We led a very quiet life, she and I together ; but she seemed perfectly happy. She was not one of those girls Ivho want excitement and dissipation. The indulgences I was able to afford her I gave her freely. She had a horse to ride, and my coachman gave her lessons. 1 gave her a bicycle, too ; and as she was fond of animals she had her dogs and her cage birds • and as the summer advanced she grew to take as deep an interest in the garden as I did myself. Bad I searched all England over I could not have found a more delightful companion. She was alvays the same—sweet, and docile, and bright. I wrote to Jack and told him about her—a little, not too much. I just said I had found a nice girl, a Miss Smith, to come and live with me. I did not want to expatiate too much on her charms, because I had other plans for my son. One day I was led to talk of these plans to Monica.

"Of course," I said, "my greatest nisli is that Colonel Somerville should marry again when ho conies home, and marry well this time. I don't mind telling you, Monica, that I have already settled in my own mind who his wife is to be." "Indeed I" she cried, rather sharply. Monica had a little trick of appearing to be startled sometimes about things which ought not to startle her in the least. She turned on me two widely-opened eyes of liquid azure. She certainly did take a real and vivid interest, dear girl, in all that concerns me.

"Who is she?" she added, quite eagerly. "Her name is Mary Nairn. She is the daughter of old friends of mine. Her parents are wealthy, and she is their only child. it would be a very good match for Jack." "And Miss Nairn herself ? Is she----"

"Oh, she is a very nice girl—handsome and clever. A little cold and reserved in her manner, perhaps, but that is no drawback, and I believe her to have been secretly attached to my son for years." "He knows her, then?"

"Oh, very well. He has known her all his life. They were children together. I think the match would have come about of itself in time. had not that actress creature come into his life."

"Do you mean that—that Colonel Somerville was in love with Miss Nairn before he met—the— the unhappy actress ?" Now 1 am a very truthful woman, and if I answered that question at all I was bound to answer it truly. "lionestiv," I replied, "I cannot say that he was. All the love was on Mary's side, but it will be different now. Ile has had his fling and paid for it. He will have sobered down and be anxious to seitle suitably, and he is quite as well aware of the advantages of this marriage as I am." One day a little later on I said to Monica: "1 think I will ask Mary Nairn to conic and stay here. I should like you to know her. Monica became most unaccountably agitated. CO-OPERATIVE AND COMMUNIST EXPERIMENTS.

In writing a short article on any subject there is always a danger of trying to cover too wide a field. Before the writer there is always the temptation to express thoughts on too many points, with the result that each is insufficiently dealt with, and the ideas conveyed are vague and many, rather than few and definite. Many volumes have been devoted to the subject of this article and in its study many men have spent the best of their lives. Obviously then, only one phase of the question can be here considered, and this will be the causes of failure in the past and the hope of success in the future. Co-operation, or the successful democratic management of an industry by the workers in that industry, is not yet an accomplished fact, therefore all attempts in that direction may be considered as partial successes only. Men have endeavoured to establish self- governing industrial organisations and after a short period of storm and strife these have either ended or reverted to the master-and- workman type. This is almost the invariable history of co-operative and communist effort, which means that the problem of industrial democracy has yet to be solved. By indus- trial democracy is here meant the manage- ment of each group of workers by themselves, and the management of each federation of groups by those groups, and so on. This must be the distinguishing feature of democracy. It is a comparatively easy thing for a number of workers to combine asl owners and to employ through their elected representatives a number of other workers. This does not involve personal restraint and the cultivation of the "sweet reasonableness" which must be necessary for democracy. When the foreman or manager of any group of workers is appointed by and responsible to sonic authority other than the workers in that group, we have a state of industrial despotism. We see then that industrial democracy is a system which will require a higher indi- vidual character in the workers constituing it. In despotism the individual is expected either to command or to obey—he is not required to co-operate on equal terms with his fellows. In democracy each worker will have two capacities, firstly as a director in determining the plan of action or policy of the group or federation of groups or combina- tion of federations ; and secondly as a worker in carrying out his detail according to that plan or policy. Obviously the individual in democracy will have to possess self-discipline and initiative—qualities which are not encouraged under the despotic typo of industry. Generally speaking,then, we may say that industrial democracy or true co-operation has not been established because a sufficient number of democrats have not yet come together. Attempts have been made to found self-governing organisations with unsuitable material. Democrats in the present system of despotism are found only here and there. The great majority of men will require training in a democratic system before they can become democrats. In the last century the Christian Socialists endeavoured to establish self-governing workshops, and this is said of them :—" The rock upon which these associations split was not want of capital or want of business experience, but difficulties in maintaining internal discipline, in preventing quarrels among men who had had little education in the ordinary sense, and whose training as wage-servants, under the lash of capitalism, had developed the meaner, rather than the nobler sides of their characters." - --

Having considered the causes of failure we come now to hopes for the future. If there have been so many hundreds of failures, even when the enthusiasm of the founders and the material conditions have favoured success, is it not hopeless to expect better of the future? This question is asked by many who sincerely desire democracy, but who think that it is beyond human nature to accomplish. For answer we must say every success is built on failures and partial successes. Undoubtedly the human race, with all its weaknesses is wiser, more humane and more tolerant than ever before. And will this progress stop? What signs are there that human intelligence has reached the limit of its development? It is true that ' greater energy, zeal or devotion cannot be shown than that of our forefathers, but this cannot be said of things intellectual. Each generation is wiser than the last because it stands on its predecessor's shoulders. Each age provides its newcomers with a better world in which to develop their faculties. If we take a number of periods in the world's his- tory we should probably (had we the means I of measuring) find that in energy and emotion there is not much to choose between one generation and another. Each period has its energetic men, its poets and its philosophers ; also its rogues, fools and weaklings. It has also its complement of ordinary men, who are neither clever nor dull, heroic nor cowardly, energetic nor lazy. But in intelligence, in knowledge of the world and of themselves, each succeeding generation makes sonic advance on the one preceding. Man is never satisfied with him- self—the desire for improvement seems to be the deepest of our instincts. In what other direction can we advance than towards industrial democracy? What else can satisfy the cravings of the masses for a higher and fuller life ? Progress will be made when a few lead where it is possible I for the masses to follow.

-- Slavery, Serfdom,Capitalism. These stages has the race passed through and each has, found the workers freer, more intelligent and more economically productive than the one before it. Can this line of advance be continued by any system other than Co-operation? I will conclude with a quotation from Herbert Spencer, the greatest analytical thinker of the 19th century : "Higher types of society are made possible only by higher types of nature. The best industrial institutions are possible only with the best of men. . . . . The requisite 'sweet reasonableness' (to use a phrase of Matthew Arnold) is not yet sufficiently prevalent. But such few co-operative bodies as survived might be the germs of a spread- ing organisation. Admission into them would be the goal of working class ambition. They would tend continually to absorb the superior, leaving outside the inferior to work as wage earners, and the first would slowly grow at the expense of the last. Obviously too, the growth would become increasingly rapid; since the master and workman typo of industrial organisation could not withstand cempetition with this co-operative type, so much more productive and costing so much less in superintendance."

W. BAGULEY.

The number in the corresponding four weeks of Ilast year was 657, showing an increase of 0, being a net decrease in 1612, to date, of 174.

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

CHIMNEY-FELLING.

I was interested in the account of the razing of the big chimney at Johnson's old soapery, Runcoro, because I happen to have witnessed st least three similar even's, and like to compare the different methods employed by one and another who specialise in this line. I have not made the acquaintance of the Liverpool steeplejack to whom the work now particu- larly in question was entrusted ; but I note that he prefers the means adopted by the majority of the members of his craft. " TIIL " SMITH. . I

I was intimate with the chief and most prominent of such men in his time— the late Mr. Smith, of Roch- dale. lie was decidedly an original. He was some- thing of a showman, being largely concerned in the running of a local circus. Moreover, he was so find of caravanning that he finally had made, at vast expense, an elaborate and ideally furnished house on wheels, and thought to make it his permanent place of abode ; but as the greater part of his time was necessarily spent in the town where his business head- quarters were, the sanitary authorities declined to sanction the arrangement so far as the borough was concerned, and the enterprising simple-lifer had to abandon his scheme and remove to an ordinary dwell- ing. The first chimney I saw him bring down was a tall stack in c Amcc ion with property which the Lancashire and Yorkshire Railway Company had acquired for the extension cf their system rand, excepting that the huge structure was of stone, the manner of it and the circumstances generally were similar to those of last week's affair at Runcorn. A RISKY BUSINESS. . .

The hazardous nature of the steeple-jack's occupa- tion is apparent to everybody, and, although Jr. Smith invariably reserved for himself the master stroke and at the supreme moment kept his assistants away from the danger zone, he of course did not incur neeelless personal risk. His successor, Mr. Forrest, follows precisely the same lines. It must he scare half-dozen years since I saw him actually at work, and he then brought down with remarkable neatness and preci-ion a tall chimney belonging to a derelict mill vvhich bad been bought for a Corporation improve- ment scheme. As a dramatic spectacle, admirably stage-managed, it resembled in all respects those which Mr. Smith was svoA to provide for the entertain- ment of expectant multitudes. By-the-bye a p:ofusely illustrated article, with a detailed description of the latter's modaa operandi, was published in the "Strand Magazine," not very long before the premature death of that lamented worthy. Mr. Helliwell, of King Cross, Halifax, is another skilful and fearless steeple- jack who has come my way as a chimney-razing expert. The first chimney stick I saw him fell cer tainly proved a most thrilling sensation. The pre- liminary preparations were of the customary sort. His men removed a quantity of masonry from the base on the side on which the shaft was to be brought down, substituting stout wooden props for the blocks of store taken out. Wiih extraordinary judgment the props were then gradually taken away in turn until the mass rested on one specially strong piece of timber. Then the chief actor stepped forward with a huge mallet, and with a tremendous blow struck away the remaining prop; then, jumping aside with wonderful agility, he " scooted" to a place of safety while the doomed chimney tumbled to earth like a house of cards ! I fancy that now-a-days, wisely as I think, Mr. Helliwell delivers the final blow by means of a dynamite charge and a time fuse. THE 20TH.

Lymm May Queen Festival fell on the day follow- ing what is known as Oak Apple D.iy. The Atatue of Charles 11, which stands in the portico of All Saints' Church, Northampton, is on that day decorated with boughs of oak and many of the townspeople wear suitable emblems in their button-ho!e3. All this is in grateful remembrar.ce of the fact that, after the great fire of 1615, the sovereign gave 1,080 tons of timber from Whittlebury Forest towards its rebuild- ing. The 25th May is stated, and pretty generally understmd to have been, "the date of the merry monarch's escape from the Parliamentary troops" ; but it was on the day following the battle of Worces- ter that he eluded the vigilance of the pursuing soldiers by climbing into the oAk at Boscobel, and the fighting took place on the 3rd September, 1651. The 29th May was the anniversary of the birth of the lucky Royal fugitive; hence the change in the date of the celebration referred to. 'THE MERRY MONTH." - - -

So much for the merry monarch. Now for the month on which we have just entered, and which in the course of three weeks will bring round the longest day. It has been observed that kings have not done over well in this month, James 111. of Scotland was murdered on the 11th, in the prime of life (36), and after a reign of 29 years ; George the First died in his carriage, a short distance from ustisburgh,in Germany at the age of OS; and the Emperor Julian—termed the Apostate—on the 27th of this month, Amongst famous battles fought wet e those of Bannockburn and Waterloo. The celebrated Duke of Marlborough,who like Alexander the Great, is said never to have lost a battle,finished the battle of life on the sixteenth of June, Other celebrities taken in June were Inigo Jones, the great architect; Joseph Addison, the essayist ; Cardinal Alberoni, the famous Prime Minister of Spain : whilst on the last day of the month General Roseno, General to king James, perpetrated sone horrible barbarities on the Irish under the walls of Londonderry. It was also in this month that the Black Bole tragedy in Calcutta took place. 'SCAT!

In the hearing of a chance acquaintance who has the misfortune to be an inveterate punster, I read the " Examiner " paragraph as to Mr. Sugars' appoint- ment under the Horbnry Urban-District Council. He immediately opened his mouth with obvious intention, and like Bunyan's pilgrim, I put my fingers in my ears and attempted to escape; but the cheerful and irrepressible idiot shouted after me that he trusted Mr. Sugars might live long to enjoy the sweets of office' Now I ask any reasonable—but there! such a follow is not worth the trouble. PROUD OF IT. He somehow reminds me of a tradesman who was awarded a prize for the worst conundrum, and was so delighted that he displayed the trophy in his shop- window. It occurred in the town in which I received my early training. One Myers erccted a temporary wooden structure for circus performances, then at the height of their popularity, and a favourite rider known as "Little Bell" topped the bill when the novel conundrum scheme was announced. The triumphant achievement was in these terms: " What is the difference between the Parish Church clock and Little Bell ?"the answer being "One rings the chimes and

the other charms the ring" Competition was keen, but this was adjudged the vilest of the vile. All the same, as I have shown, the winner was mightily proud of it. EMPIRE DAY.

May I have my belated little say as to this—or rather may I briefly cite Mr. 11. G. Wells on the subject? I will remember bow, in the first number of " Even body's Weekly," he had a characteristic article on " Cement of Empire," in which he pointed out that the British Empire has no common economic interests and no natural common enemy. It 13 not adapted to any form of Zollverein or any form of united aggression. Visibly, on the map of the world it has a likeness to open hands, while the German Empire—except for a few ill-advised and imitative colonies—is clenched into a central European unity. SINGULARLY APPROPRIATE,

I trust I may be permitted a further timtly quota- tion from this most useful contribution. Mr. Wells Proceeded —Physically, our Empire is incurably scattered, various and dividtd, and it is to quite other links and forces, it seems to me, than fiscal or military unification that we who desire its continuance must look to hOld it together. There never was anything like it before. Essentially, it is an adventure of the British spirit, sanguine, di-cursive. and beyond coin- parisoa insubordinate, adaptable and originating. It has been made 'by odd and irregular means, by trading companies, pioneers, explorers, unauthorised seamen, adventurers like Clive, eccentrics like Gordon, invalids like Rhodes. It has been made, in spite ot authority and officialdom, as no other empire was ever made. The nominal rulers of Britain never planned it; it happened almost in spite of them. Their chief contribution to its history has been the loss of the United States. It is a living thing that bas arisen, not a dead thing put together. Beneath the thin legal and administrative ties that hold it together lies the far more vital bond of a traditional free spontaneous activity. It has a common medium of expressi )n in the English tongue, a unity of liberal and tolerant purpose amidst its enormous variety of localised life and cohur. And it is in tho development and strengthening, the enrichment, the rendering more conscious and more purposeful, of that broad creative spirit of the British that the true cement and continu- ance of ur Empire is to be found. The writer's argument, put into a nutshell, as that the Empire must live by the forc a that begot it.

TO ROAD BOOS AND MOTOR FIENDS.

A re6ent extremely painful fatality at Warrington, respecting which I have DO desire to add a single word of comment, reminded me of certain remarks made by Mr. Burdett Coutts. Ml'., at his sale of hackney horses at Bowden, East Yorkshire, not very long ago. Very truly, and fairly enough, be said that motor-cars were a convenience, and had come to be a necessity. Properly used, and paying a proportion of the cast of road maintenance, their future was certain. He had one himself which he hoped was properly used. Frankly, he did not like the picture of England suddenly turned into a nation sitting back comfortably in motors, goggle-eyed, smoking big cigars choking the humble wayfarer with clouds of dust, looking very luxurious and very disdainful as they trashed along slaying chickens and maiming dogs, and now and then making marmalade of someone else, or of themselves all for the purpose of getting there as quickly as possible. sil3le. iGOOD OLD GEE-GEE.

It is a relief to tur—n—Conit-iii-,; foregoing to thoughts suggested. by the Warrington Horse Parade. As the authority Just quoted asked on the cccasion to which I have alluded : What luxury could be greater than a beautiful pair of English harness horses, finely matched and mannered? There was no such absurd sight (he further declared) as a fins lady decked out in silks ani satins, with art enormous hit of thelatest fashion, going slowly up and down the pirk on an engine. - However, as I myself happened to be writing in another place while the local parade was in pro- gress, in these days of petrol-driven conveyances, it is agreeable to note the keen interest in horseflesh still manifested at the cattle shows—yearly gatherings, be it remembered, not of agriculturalists and breeders exclusively, but of all sorts and conditions of men and women. Although the general demand for horseflesh is by no means what it used to be, and the discourage- ment of the profession of rearing, owing to the ever- increasing motor traffic, has bad the effect of sending up market prices, it is pleasant to think that the average Lancashire or Cheshire man retains all his old affectiox for the nob'est quadruped in theso or any of the other English counties. • THE TRIUMPH OF NATURE.

Life is nct mere existence, lut the enjoyment of good health. To enjoy your health it is necessary to live according to the mks and dicates of Nature. First in importance concerning our habits is the matter of diet, It is no use partly to ruin the digestive organs, and then try to repair the damage by means of drugs. Live properly and choose the right kind of diet. Have you ever taken the trouble to study the relative values of the various foodstuffs ? Perhaps not. You may be amongst those who think that man can live by beef alone. If so, it will interest you to know that the little Greek Currant contains four times as much nutriment as lean beef. Likewise that Currants are absolutely unique as a perfect and pure food. They are nearly all grape sugar, the most nutritious and sustaining of all food. Athletes and others who are famed for their feats of endurance do not eat huge quantities of meat; they prefer Currant bread, or any other preparation in which Currants appear. Apart from the habits of athletes, there are the pronouncements of eminent men of science who are constantly urging us to eat Currants, not on occasions but regularly. The frequent use of Currants means consistent good health, and not mere existence, marred by the displeasures of illness. The adoption of a liberal diet of Currants is the shortest road to robust health.

SERIOUS ACCIDENY AT PEARSON AND KNOWLES COLLIERY. WORK STOPPED FOR A MONTH.

- A rather serious winding acaident occurred at No. 4 pit of Messrs. Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Company, Ince, on Saturday. Twelve men were being wound to the surface when the drumkhaft of a large pair of engines broke. The cage was then aboulafty yards from the surface. The engine-man jealising the position, at once brought the engine to a standstill. Mr. John Knowles, general marmger, and several other officials bad a temporary erection made for winding the men to the top. This was carried out in about three hours' time. About 150 more men were also in the Arley Mine waiting to be brought up. Thy were wound up by another shaft, and Mr. Knowles at once had the furnace damped down. A feed of fresh air was diverted into the upcast shaft, where the twelve men were suspended in the cage. The mishap will render further work in the shaft impossible for a month, and 400 men will be affected.

===== 0002601_19120601_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
THE STRENGTH OF INSECTS.

I dare say we have all of us at times been surprised at the strength of an insect. Take up a large beetle for instance and try to hold it; you find that you need great strength to bold it between your fingers, The best way to realize the muscular power of a beetle is to make the comparison of its size and notice how many times its own weight it can carry.

A-man would be considered strong if he could drag a mass weighing three or four ' times as much as himself, yet a beetle will walk with five hundred times its own weight. If a man were placed under a wooden box with five times his weight upon the top to hold it down, he would remain there until somebody came to release him, but to retain, say, a stag beetle and keep him a prisoner we should have to pile on top of the box at least eighteen hundred times its weight. Just think of that! And then just test the power of a beetle's nippers. Try to keep him a prisoner and then when he gets angry let him take a nip at your finger, but be sure you put gloves on, and let them be very strong gloves too, of leather if possible.

A HAPPY LITTLE POEM.

I happened to find the other day a cheery little poem. Curiously en, ugh I found it while it was raining and at a moment when the rain was keeping me indoors against my wishes. It was written by Robert Loveman in 1882 and will be good to turn back too if we ever get annoyed (which I hope we never do) because of the rain. Someone was com- plaining very bitterly about rain and Mr. Loveman gave him an answer by writing ;

THE RAIN OF THE ROSES. " It is n't raining rain to me, It's raining daffodils ; In every dimpled drop I see Wild dowers on the hills. The clouds of gray engulf the day And overwhelm the town; It is n't raining rain to naq, It's raining rose, down. It is n't raining rain to me, But fields of clover bloom, Where every buccaneering bee, May find a bed and room ; A health unto the happy, A fig to him who hots, It is n't raining rain to me, It's raining violets." PATIENCE. How smooth the sea-beach pebbles are! But - do you know? The ocean worked a hundred years To make them so? And I once saw a little girl Sit down and cry Because she could not cure a fault With one small "try." THIS WEEK'S STORY.

"Thomas, you have disobeyed your old grandmother." "No, I didn't, ma." "Yes, you did. Have you not been swimming ?" "Yes, ma." "Didn't I hear her say to you not to go swimming ?" "0, she didn't tell us that. She only came out and said, 'Boys, I wouldn't go swimminV and I shouldn't think she would, an old rheumatic woman like her.

THE WARSHIPS RETURN.

EARLY MORNING SCENES AT LATCHFORD.

The cruiser Bristol and the destroyers which accom- panied her to Manchester left the Trafford Wharf in the small hours on Tuesday morning. Before leaving the vessels displayed their searchlights over the docks. The destroyers left first, and the Bristol, taken by two tugs, proceeded to the locks about three o'clock. The vessels passed through Latchford locks at intervals from 430 to G.3o—in fact they were reported clear of the Ship Canal shortly after ten o'clock. They passed the Liverpool Lauding-stage at a good rate of speed, and were quickly lost to view. The cruiser was the last boat to leave the river, having stopped at Runcorn to adjust the mast, which had previously to be lowered in passing under a canal bridge. The warship excited considerable interest among the crowds of people who were on the landing-stage. During their stay at Manchester the warships were visited by about 1,250,000 people. On Monday alone 50,000 people went to Old Trafford Wharf to see the vessels, and the crush was so great that the police had to close the gates.

===== 0002601_19120601_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
AT LATCHFORD LOCKS.

An "Examiner," representative was among the early risers on Tuesday morning and enjoyed the unique (for him) experience of seeing the sun rise over i the placid waters of the Ship Canal. Truth to tell t was a prettier sight than one usually sees when scanning the upper reaches of the turgid waterway. But there were people out and about before " Old Sol " himself. We found quite a small crowd at Latchford Locks when we sauntered up at 4 a.m., including at least three enthusiastic snap-shottists. Everybody was in a gay mood, notwithstanding the 1 early [rising and meagre apology for breakfast which' I most of those present had to be content with. Nor was the waiting long. Before .4 30 there was a suspicious blur on the horizon Manchester way, and then the gates of the small lock began to tremble as though an earthquake had occurred. The cause was not obvious until the leading destroyers hove in sight, and then we found that the Zulu and Amazon were indulging in a modest speed trial on the Thelwall " measured mile." They were not doing their full 35 knots—of course—or there would have been an inundation as well as an apprehensive trembling of the lock gates. But the pace these small, powerful vessels displayed was sufficient to disturb the waters for a goodly distance both ahead and in the rear, and to alarm the officials in charge at Latchford. Hasty words of caution were poured on the advance guard, who wirelessed back, and the pace of the following vessels was considerably subdued. The period of waiting for the arrival of the cruiser " Bristol " was anything but tiresome, as a wag from Manchester way engaged all and sundry in political argument and the fun fast waged and furious. Woe betide the unlucky Tory who ventured to cross swords with this old campaigner ! Soon after 030 the cruiser steamed majestically into the locks,and everyone crowded round for a word with the Jack-tars who lined the rails. Most of them seemed far from fit for the fray (if there bad been ole imminent). Manchester's welcome must have been particularly effusive ! A sailor nearest the writer confessed to having -bad no sleep throughout the week-end! He was looking forward to a spell of quiet and regular hours when the ship resumed her patrol round the Orkneys.

Sykes, M.P., on Saturday laid the fouodation-stones. —Speaking at the lunation, Lord Egerton said be was glad to have been given the opportunity to enter upon a commercial speculation. (Laughter.) He did not believe in too much philsnthropy. (Hear, hear.) He had gone into the question, and he did not think that be would be out of pocket. (Hear, hear.) Having premises such as they would have, they would get a greatly increased membership. Colonel Sykes said he would always endeavour to carry on the traditions which the division held, and if it were necessary to look upon some things with the " modern " eye, he hoped his modern eye, at any rate, would be able not entirely to disregard the traditions of the past. (Applause).—ln laying his foundation- stone, Lord Egerton said he thought he was improving the place by having pulled down a certain number of cottages which he honestly confessed he was asham ed of owning. (" No, no"). He had put up a number of better cottages, and so no-one was any the worse. (Ile3r, hear).

DEATH OF MRS.. J. R. PICKMERE

A Former Mayoress of Warrington.

Warringtonians in general, but especially those of the older generation, have heard with deep regret of the death of Mrs. J. R. Pickmere who passed away at her residence. Cedar Lawn, Tbelwall, shortly belre midnight on Friday of last week., Owing to increas- ing weakness, the deceased lady, who had reached a ripe old age, had not been seen in Warrington for a considerable time, but the diligent and self-sacrificing work which she did for the town and its institutions in years gone by, will be long and gratefully remem- bered. Mrs. Pickmere was at one time very closely associated with the civic life of the borough, for she was the Mayoress of Warrington in 1873-4,1870-80-1- 2, and part of the year 1887, her husband, the late Major John Richarl Pickmere, having the honour to hold the office of Mayor for a longer period than anyone who has acted as chief magistrate since Warrington was incorporated in 1817. Major and Mrs. Pickmere brought to the performance of their high duties a degree of tact and personal charm which won general admiration—in fact they were an ideal Mayor and Mayoress, and were never happier than when promoting those successful civic functions which were such a notable and apprecia(ed feature of their terms of office. The eminent services which Major Pickmere rendered were recognized by the Council when they conferred upon him the honorary freedom of the borough. His death, which took place on October 9th, 1890, was felt as a distinct loss to the municipality. Mrs. Pickmere was greatly devoted to the work of the Infirmary and Dispensary and her example of consistent and generous support was an inspiration to the members cf the Ladies' Committee of which she was life president. The institution had no more sincere friend, whose willing help could always be counted upon. Although the Infirmary had the first claim upon her, she did much to assist other charitable institutions. She also interested herself in the affairs of the village of Thelwall, and was a liberal supporter of All Saints' Church and schools. For many years Mrs. Pickmere was a member of the Warrington Dramatic Society, and figured with success in some .of its best- remembered productions. Mr. E. Ralph Pickmere (town clerk of Liverpool), and Mr. W. Travers Pickmere (wine merchant, Warring'on and Nantwrch) are stepsons of the deceased lady.

PROSPECTS OF VINE CULTURE IN CANADA.

A Canadian correspondent writes :—" It does not seem to be sufficiently appreciated in England that tbe climatic conditions and soil over large areas in Canada have been demonstrated to be as highly adapted for vine culture as the best-known vineyard districts in Europe which have a world-wide nputation. Fruit growing in Canada in the popular mind is associated with the raising of apples, pears and the hardier fruits It is true, grape growing at present is perhaps of local rather than of national importance ; but of the possi- bilities to be reali=ed in the cultivation of the vine in Canada there can be no question. Grape growing centres in the province of Ontario, whose vineyards cover 12 000 acres. This province has a larger variety of local climate than any other country of similar extent in the world, due to the inficencs of the great lakes and to the hydrographic system of the interior. Be twren the north shore:of Lake Superior and Lake Erie the summer varies from those of Scotland to those of Central and much of Southern Francs—summers as warm as in Northern Italy and warmer than the coast of Portugal. The most favoured sections lie along the shores of Lake Ontario and Lake Erie, including the , Niagra Peninsula. " Aceording to expert 'opinion, there is an area of some 9,000 square miles in Ontario where even peaches of one kind or other can be successfully grown in the open air, while in the valley of the St. Lawrence there is an area of probably 30,000 square miles where the soil and clime are suited for the vine. The Niagara district and the counties along Lake Erie are the natural home of the famous "Concord" grape vine, the variety with which the vineyards of France and Italy and other countries have been re-stocked after the ravages caused by phylloxsra. In this region, the counties of Lincoln, Wentworth and Welland are mainly identified with grape growing and have a total annual output of from 20,000 tons. The vine begins bearing at sour years and continues for a lifetime. The vines are grown on trellis supports. Good grape land is plentiful. In the most favoured districts it may be purchased at from £l6 an acre. "The figures furnished by the Ontario Government of the cost of maintenance and the profit per acre of vineyard are as follows :—Cost of bringing an acre of grapes into full bearing, including supports for vines, Et) ; annual outlay per acre when bearing (pruning and tying, cultivation, spraying three times, pinking and packages and delivery to station), £l2; average yield three tons or 750 baskets, average price at £23 (a yield of five tons per acres is frequently obtained); net return per acre, £ll.

THE BASER SORT.

- BY WINIFRED GRAHAM;

Author of "A Strange Solution," "The Beautiful Mrs. Loch," "The Star-Child," "On the Down_Grade," "The Beautiful Schemer," &C. I.

Sydney lltiogerford, a young English dodo), had been studying abroad for some months, picking up what knowledge he could on the Continent and interesting himself in science.

As yet he was only on the borderland of a career; full of enthusiasm, and intensely ambitious. Not alone were the ills of humanity stirring him to work and effort—possibly the memory of a sweet face in England, a pair of blue eyes, a graceful girlish figure, had something to do with the young man's energy. It was of Veronica he thought, as the train carrying him to Calais whirled the dreamer through space. Surely she was beside him, a phantom form, smiling hopefully, assuring him of future success. And yet they were only friends ; no word of love had been spoken, though Sydney's eyes may have told a tale. Veronica, he knew, must marry a man worthy of her, one who could provide the comforts of life. For this very reason he yearned after riches, and keenly desired the world's wealth. As he pondered on this fair image of the ideal woman, he suddenly became aware of a figure opposite, who had entered his carriage at the previous station—a hospital nurse, and evidently English. He could not see her face, hidden behind a huge handkerchief, with which she mopped her eyes vigorously. Apparently she was suffering. He watched her a few moments in silence, then ventured a remark:

"I am afraid you have hurt your eye. Can I help you ? I'm a doctor." She removed the handkerchief quickly, and I el• face, though well-modelled, and by no means plain, did not impress hint favourably. It had a sinister expression, and the narrow, tightly-compressed lips indicated a hard nature. She wore her dark hair parted on her forehead; she spoke in a low, gnat voice. "Thank you. I must have got a piece of grit or coal in it. I should indeed be grateful if you could do anything—but the train is so elalry--" lie bent forward, ar d, taking a pencil out of his pocket, rolled back the lid, dexterously removing the torturing atom. This led the way to friendly intercourse, and Nurse Dora, otherwise Miss Bentley, kept the ball of conversation rolling, telling him ninny incidents of interest in her professional career. "I have been travelling abroad with a patient," she said, "who, under my care, completely recovered, which is fortunate, as I am now on my way to a sick friend, whose case is a very strange one. She is an enormously wealthy woman, a widow, and childless—Madame Pollette byname, a miser by nature. I feel quite sure she is dying, alone and friendless, without medical aid." Nurse Dora, in spite of her sinister looks, had a strange way of attracting interest. Madame Pollette's illness was one upon which there were many discussions in medical circles, an uncommon ailment, which Sydney Ilimgerford was anxious , to make his special study. With great tact, Nurse Dora refrained at first from telling him what was in her mind; and not until they had nearly reached the station at which she must alight did she spring her request upon him: Would he not break his journey and come with her to Madame Pollette's house If—tc Madame Pollette—he would call lihnself Nurst Dora's friend, all would be well. He might at !east ensure the invalid a more peaceful end, even if his knowledge of the disease proved ineffectual to save her life. It would not only be a philan- thropic action, but interesting from a scientific standpoint. Thus she urged her r 'quest, and Sydney's enter- prising nature prompted him to fall in with the plan.' So it came to pass that instead of speeding on to Calais, the young doctor found himself driving with Nurse Dora through t tracts of unknown country to a tumble-down chateau, surrounded with trees. A toothless old beg opened the door and conducted them across the hall. Sydney noticed the wallpaper was peeling off in ragged ribbons, the damp dripped through the ceiling. The depressing .s.tmosphere of the place seemed to strike him with it cold chill. Ile thought of Veronica, and shuddered at the prevailing gloom. For this he had postroned the meeting with her— for this!

1 r.

Madame Toilette's case, and her strange. altrac- tiie personality, engrossed the young doctor's attention so completely that he no longer regretted having delayed his return. The invalid, a wan yet beautiful woman, lay in a critical condition, having hitherto been waited upon solely by the old woman with the toothless gums. Dr. Hungerford's presence seemed to exert a magic influence over her at once, and her gratitude for his services proved so touching in its fervour and sincerity, that he often felt quite uncomfort- able at receiving such an amount of praise and devotion. Every day Madame Follette implored him afresh not to desert her, and, as far as the patient was concerned, Sydney felt no desire to leave. But Nurse Dora troubled him ; she had adopted a tone of familiarity and overwhelming friendliness, which he in no way reciprocated. Seldom was she absent from his side, and her presence filled him with irritation. The dying woman may have seen something of this ; anyhow, she often sent for him, and asked Nurse Dora to leave them together. She would talk of her illness, of small economies, , and bewail the prevalent extravagance of the up-to- date femme du monde. "Most women," she told him, "have little or no idea of preserving their fortunes ; men with business heads—and especially professional men—are the most fitted to deal prudently with large sums of money. I have always thought, Dr. Hungerford, that 1 should leave my fortune to a man." "Really," he said, soothingly. "Yes, a man of stamina and talent—above all, a charitable man. I understand from Nurse Dora that you came here with no idea of being paid— you are making no charge for your services; you have relieved my pains, and waited upon me like a brother or aeon, with no idea of pecuniary profit." He was obliged to bashfully acknowledge this was the case. He did not like to say that through his so-called charity he was gaining experience useful Lo his profession. "I 2 the door shut ?" she asked. "Are you sure nobody is listening?" He went to the door, and convinced her that anything she might say to him would not be over- heard by a third person. "You see," she whispered, "I want to warn you against Nurse Dora—a scheming, untrustworthy woman. You may think I am ungrateful, but indeed—indeed, I know her too well, and I speak the truth from my heart, she is here merely as a carrion seeking prey ; she forgets that I am fully acquairrted with several very shady incidents in her career ; she hopes I may be fool enough to make her my heiress. Hardly a day passes but she lends up to the subject • she has fixed her affections upon you, she speaks already as if souse secret understanding existed between you. Tell me, if you can, honestly, that her insinuations are merely lies ? She was never truthful, and I can't believe--" Sydney Hungerford listened in horror ; he burst out angrily, protesting in all good faith, that no power on earth would ever induce him to marry Nur,e Dora. Ills ill-concealed disgust at the suggestion seemed to delight his patient, "Then," she said, "I can now decide how to act. I will leave a small sum of money to Dora, sufficient to repay her for the trouble of nursing me, and the bulk of my fortune I shall bequeath to you. But how am I to be quite sure that after my death this double-faced woman does not entrap you into marriage ?" Sydney drew out a pocket-bcok and took from its folds the portrait of a girl with the sweetest face -Madame Pollette had ever seen. Across the photo-

..... graph wa writ'en the name "Veronica." "Ali 1" murmured Madame Toilette. "So this is your defence against the wiles of Dora Bentley! Well, I am satisfied. You have been very good to me, and I want to repay you." Sydney llungerford thanked her heartily for her kind intentions towards him, at the same time meriting her he had by no means despaired of restoring her to health. But at this she merely smiled sadly, murmuring she did not want life, only freedom from min while consciousness lasted. ror some days Nydney fought the dread enemy, and did all in his power to save the life whose death would bring him gain. A few friends came to say good-bye, and one or two men of business

called, but no one seemed really to care. It was a atrange end for a woman who had been beautiful. Nurse Dora did all in her power to find out what arrangements Madame Toilette had made for the disposal of her wealth, but it was kept a pro- found secret.

The invalid hated talking of her money, and to the last moment threw out no hint upon the subject. So, still in ignorance, Dora Bentley closed Madame Pollette's eyes and pulled clown the blinds of the bare, comfortless bed-chamber containing the lifeless body of the woman-miser.
