When the contents of the will were Made known, Nurse Dora appeared thunderstruck. She had folly expected to inherit everything in return for her services voluntarily offered to Madame Follette. Instead ;dm found herself merely entitled to a small legacy, sufficient to keep her from actual poverty for the rest of her life. That Dr. Ifungerford should be the favoured one cut her deeply, seeing she had introduced him. Yet, after reflection, she came to the conclusion things might have been worse. "Surely," she argued to herself, "the least he could do under the circumstances would be to offer me his name." Did ho not ()we her this marvellous good fortune ? And were they not now on such intimate terms of friendliness as frequently deepened into sentiment and love ? She had been attracted by his handsome face and pleasant manners from the first, and it was chiefly with the idea of conquering the young doctor and winning his affections that she asked him to accompany her to Madame Pollette's lonely dwelling. She was also anxious for him to be present as a witness when Madame Follette died. A morbid terror had filled her mind lest she should be suspected of hastening the death by which she hoped to benefit. Now everything had turned out differently to her desires. Sydney Ifungerford was unresponsive and apparently blind to her charm., but what proved till more vexing the fortune had also flitted to him. "I suppose," she said, "you have no objection to escorting Inc to England ? We may as well travel ioeel "

Be bowed politely, Ms manner towards her - had stiffened of late, lie avoided her, and talked little when they met. With the thought of home came the thrilling delight of meeting Veronica, the girl he had loved always, the girl he could now afford to marry and keep in luxury. • "It strikes me," said Sister Dora's gruff voice, "that you owe a good deal to a chance meeting in train."ft

"Yes, indeed. I hope you do not think lam ungrateful either to the Fates or to yourself." The woman's dark eyes gleamed, her hard face somewhat relaxed.

"It isn't exactly gratitude we women want," she said, "from the men we have helped. 1 knew from your face you were ambitious the first moment I set eyes on you. I thought if Madame Toilette could see him she would make him her heir.' I guessed you wanted riches more than any- thing in the world. Quite young doctors are not generally millionaires, especially travelling second- class. So I said inwardly: I will be the stepping- stone upon which he shall rise.' I divined it all. lam clairvoyante and see into the future. The first move was to get you to the house, the next to work upon Madame Pollette's feelings. I suggested she should leave you money ; I pointed out your talents ; I, who but for the unselfish devotion I lavished on your cause, might myself have come into my old friend's possessions!" Sydney listened amazed to the long string of lies. Did the woman take him for a fool ? Had she not plainly shewn her disgust at the reading of the will ? Was it possible now she expected him to believe in her disinterested motives ? He realised her aim at once. As he turned his eyes on the tall, defiant figure he knew she was all but asking him to make her his wife. But since there are none so blind as those who will not see, Sydney Hungerford merely answered with a conventional smile.

"I thank you sincerely for your kind thought of me. I have been favoured far beyond my deserts. But on one point you must allow me to undeceive you. Madame Pollette told me herself she had always wished to leave her fortune to a man. She had small faith in the thriftiness of the fairer sex." "Oh, so you discussed the matter with her l" broke in Nurse Dora, sharply. "Why did you never tell me ? Ido not feel I have been treated fairly. You gained an undue influence over your patient, and in my opinion there is but one repara- tion that you can offer, one way of proving your gratitude to me, one reward to repay what I have done for yon." "Really I " he said. "It would be interesting to hear your views on the subject." From his tone it was difficult to judge whether he spoke seriously or sarcastically. Very handsome he looked as lie turned his eyes calmly upon her with an expression of scrutiny. She tried to read his thoughts, but found the task impossible ; her self-control was slowly slipping away. At last, after a pause in which she felt he must hear the beating of her heart, she burst out passionately: "Oh, how short-sihted you are! Do you want everything spelt? You might have seen from the first. You must guess now wlfat I mean but can- not say. And yet, why should it not be said, especially in these days of equal rights for men and women? The reparation you owe me can be expressed in a single word—' marriage ' I have helped you to success and fortune. Now that you are rich through ins, will you leave your tool at the bottom of the ladder? I think not. Ingratitude is an offence against honour, a crime amongst gentlemen. I have stooped low to teach you your duty, don't let me feel my words have been in vain. Give me your promise that I shall be your wife." She was !peaking now in a whisper, and her hand wits on his shoulder—a large capable hand with ill4mpt nails. Her eyes shot, fire and a deep flush flooded cheek and brow. She bent as if to receive an expected embrace. He recoiled as from the touch of a serpent. "Enough l" he said. "I have listened too long to your folly. it is one of the hardlit tasks a man could perform, to refuse a proposal trom a woman, but in your case I must say the proposal is merely a barter, and you ask for marriage (so sacred a thifig!) in return for a benefit conferred. Now on stand, I refuse ever again to discuss such matters. I whold that your claim is unjust and absurd. You implored me, for some reason quite apart from the one given to-day, to accompany you to Madame Pollette's house. In doing so I was conferring a favour on you, for at the time I never contemplated reaping the very poorest harvest for my pains. 1 own it turned out a singularly fortunate journey. Yon, too, are well paid if I am much overpaid. Things are not always equally divided in this world." • anNc,rivirseeTeitrant itotui turned e(sl h eheerrostdk the himom, withoe

the door, and slammed it behind her vigorously. Sydney caught a glimpse of her face, dark with fury as she vanished from his sight. "That woman will make a bad enemy," he said, "but better have her as an enemy than a wife, and a choice between the two." IV.

Sydney was not slow to tell Veronica of his good fortune, and on his return to Landon the first letter he opened on reaching home was a note from her congratulating him on his wonderful luck. "For yon," she wrote, "I am so very, very glad —for myself, perhaps, just a little sorry. You will seem such a 'big- man ' now, that I shall feel I have lost my 'playmate.' He will be alvtays in request., this rich Dr. Hungerford, and I shall not dare send for him at unconventional hours, to ask his advice about all my own silly little affairs. But anything resembling a grumble at such a time, from a friend, must sound like heresy in your ears! I want to congratulate you in person, not with a tard steel pen, but a hearty handshake." He pressed the letter to his lips. How the sight of the writing thrilled him ! With what joy he read the contents! What could she think he wanted with his money but her happiness? That same evening he would call on her, and, if an opportunity offered, the words should be said that would put all doubt of him for ever out of her mind. Veronica lived alone with her father—a studious old man, and a philosopher. As likely as not, he would be locked in his study, and, in this case, what more favourable moment-- Sydrey did not hesitate long. Hastily swallowing his dinner and discarding his usual smoke, he drove to the house in Regent's Park which held his dearest earthly treasure. • Truly, the gods seemed on his side! Cupid must have planned the meeting, so absolutely did actual events tally with his previous hopes. Mr. Tempest was very busy, and had given orders no one must disturb him • hut Miss Temnest nits practising in the bumloii , Mr. Ilungerfuret cared to come in. . Mr. Hungerford did care—a zood deal more than

he would nave acknowledged to anyone pet then, but Veronica herself. He could hear her singing as he crossed the balk "What a sweet, melodious voice ! " he thought. "So exquisitely in tune, so deliciously sympathetic!' The boudoir door opened, the music abruptly ceased. His brain was in a whirl, but he faintly heard—as if far away—his own name being announced; then at last—at last—he was in Veronica's presence. How adorable she looked in the simple white evening gown, her blue eyes sparkling with pleasure, her hand outstretched welcomingly. "I never expected to see you to-night," she said. "It is indeed good of you to come so soon." They stood for a moment in silence looking at each other, their faces radiant. "Good—to.please myself ?" he replied, lightly. "I could not wait till to-morrow, that was why I came."

"And you look just the saute, after all your astonishing advent tires," she declared ; "not a bit more important, not even a little taller I Do tell me what you did to make the old lady leave you all that money ?" "I attended her free of charge. She was a miser, you know. But now I have to scold you first of all for something in your letter. Perhaps• you can guess what offended me ?" "Oh, about not sending for you at all sorts ot times ! You know father used to call you my 'handy-man.' Do you remember the day you put up the pictures ?—it took is whole afternoon." 'Remember I" he said. "Why, I remember every day I ever spent with you. The recollections of those liappy hours kept me from feeling terribly home-sick abroad, and your little portrait, that was never absent from me for a moment." "The portrait you stole," she retorted, laughingly. "I don't believe I ever gave it to you." "But I asked if I might steal it first. Give me my due I" "Well, I daresay you did!" "But now," he persisted, "if you care for me, Veronica, as I care for you—if—if—you could ever love me, there need be no thefts, darling— darling--" He had strained her to his heart, and was kissing her passionately. She did not try to draw away, only an expression of deep joy and peace catne into her eyes. "Sydney, I have always loved you. Didn't you know ? " "I dared not guess. • I was afraid of misleading myself." "BM," she added, quickly, "I thought you cared for someone else. I was told you were going to marry Nurse Dora." He started at the name. "Who told you that ? Why, she was the woman who took me to Madame Toilette. We both attended the poor lady profes- sionally, and subsequently reaped rewards. But personally I disliked Nurse Dora intensely." "Don't look so annoyed, Sydney. I am not jealous in the least; but I have heard a good deal about her lately from Dr. Bonsor." "Who is Dr. Bonsor ? A new friend?"

"Yes. I seem to run to doctors, don't I ? with a mischievous twinkle in her eyes. "He is Nurse Dora's brother-in-law, a widower. She stays with 'him when she is not nursing. Ile says he lives in London. You would call it the suburbs —it's somewhere beyond Vauxhall. He keeps a few lunatics at his house, and he tells me Nurse Dora has a wonderful power over mad people." "Well, thank goodness, I am not mad!" declared Sydney. 'Please never accuse me again of being in love with that terrible woman. I ought to be grateful to her, but instead, her very name gives me a shudder. Strange you should have met a relation of hers. She must have told some awful crackers in her letters to this Dr. Bonsor. Was he—like the rest—in love with you? It seems a chronic disease that attacks everyone who corc..s to this house." "Don't be silly!" "But you have not answered my question. - "Well, I can't sly he was not; but I never cared for him. I wouldn't have married him for all the world, and I begged him not to come again —his presence worried me." "What an odd coincidence," said Sydney, "that I should be connected with the sister-in-law. For Lime future, we had better both avoid that couple. Our engagement will be announced directly, and then Dr. Bonsor will know that the little fairy tales told him by Nurse Dora had no foundation, and that his attentions in this direction will be strongly resented."

• Though Sydney spoke lightly, he was at heart somewhat troubled by the distastefdl memory of Nurse Dora. \Vas it not possible that she and her brother-in-law together might hatch some desperate scheme of revenge, to try and hinder Veronica's happiness ? "What sort of a man is Dr. Bonsor ?" he asked later, when they had discussed their future, indulging in lovers' rhapsodies to their hearts' content. "lie isn't bad-looking, but it is a face I should never tru 4. Father was quite in a fuss for fear I should want to marry him. It seems men never like Dr. Bonsor, though ho is quite popular with women."

.... Sydney said no'hing, but changed the subject to pleasanter topics. He did not refer again either t,- Pr. Bonsor or Nurse Dora. But that night, sfter he had parted with loving words from Veronica, her sweet face was absent from his dreams, and instead the sinister visages of Dora Bentley and an unknown man troubled Sydney's sleeping hours. Ile fancied their lips whispered horrible things, they appeared to him out of a great black cloud. When he tried to right them, each turned into a bat, with immense wings, and whirled past him, making the air s!rangely cold. He woke with his heart beating furiously, and his mind full of death !

When the engagement was announced between Sydney and Veronica, Miss Bentley, on the strength of Dr. Bonsor's friendship and her own s,cquaintance with Sydney Ilungerford, called on Miss Veronica Tempest to formally tender con- gratulations. So charming was she in her manner, that the young girl was quite touched, especially when remembering Dr. Bonsor's disappointment. "I do think it was so kind of you to come," mid the pretty fiancee, as Nurse Dora took her leave. "I feel I shall always owe you a debt of gratitude for introducing Sydney to Madame Pollette."

"That was just a chance piece of fortune, my dear," she replied. "But I, on the other hand, liave to thank you for leaving me my good, generous brother-in-law. So long as he remains unmarried, I can always reel I have a home. He told me Of his affection for you, and that you had ,•efuied him. Poor fellow ! Though I sympathise in his suffering, I yet tell him that selfishly I rejoice. He is so good and noble—a perfect angel to me." Veronica could not help wondering, as Nurse Dora pressed an affectionate kiss upon her cheek at parting, why Sydnay had not liked so exceedingly amiable a woman. She might have wondeted less, could she have seen the expression on Dora Bentley's face as she Ftrode away from the house, or heard the internal mutterings of her late visitor's mind: "So that was the viper he prefers to use, who would have shed my heart's blood to win his affection ! That pale-faced doll of a a thing, the girl upon whom Alfred Bonsor seeks revenge—and lie shall have it, too, if I can give it him. Ah, Miss Veronica, you think you are ,on very firm ground—but you do not know the strength of your fors I Bonsor is a inan of science, he means to bind your will to his, to carry you away to. dis'ant lands —far away from those who can help you, as his prisoner, his wife! He is tired of his life in England ; he has accepted a post in a wild Indian island, where 3-on, with your doll's beauty, will be a constant delight and joy to Min. You won't know your own mind, Miss Veronica, when you jilt 'Sydney Hungerfori for a former lover, and fly the country with Alfred Bonsor !" She laughed --a low, cynical laugh—as she thought of the terrible plot she had hatched with Alfred. What a poor thing the human brain was, after all, easily governed by man's skill I By means of strong drugs, Alfred assured her, it was possible to brim, the most perverse man or woman to a state of acquiescence. He was certain if Dora could lure Veronica alone to his house the plan could be arranged successfully. Certain preparations were in readiness by which she would t ecome docile as a lamb. Everything had been well puzzled out, each detail planned with extraordinary forethought and precision.

Sydney was distinctly annoyed that Dora Bentley should have dared to call upon his fiancee. "1 shall hear some more about Dr. Bonsor to- day." he told Veronica one mornium "as I ama going to drive on a motor-ear to the Iteen Ultd) this afternoon with a friend of his. I hear the mad patients are to be given up. One has grown extremely vicious lately, and I fancy she frightened Ronson We pass his house on our way. lie is going to look out purposely, as lie's interested in motors." .

That afternoon Veronica found herself thinkird, somesfiat frequently el Dr. Bonsor, wonderin whether it was the motor-car or Sydney he most wished to see. She was returning from a shopping expedition when Nurse Dora arrested her on the doorstep. "You must come at once," she said, laying her hand firmly on Veronica's arm, "there has been an accident. Mr. Hungerford was thrown from the car and carried insensible to my brother-in-law's house. Alfred is attending him, and insisted upon my fetching you. Ile does not think the injuries are serious, so be a brave girl and try not to break down." White as a sheet, and trembling at the news, Veronica followed her into a small brougham and allowed herself to be driven away. Many were the anxious questions with which she plied Nurse Dora, to all of which enconraging answers were given. "Keep very calm, don't excite yourself, or Alfred may not let you see him," said Dora Bentley, as they neared Vauxhall. "We had to carry Mr. Hungerford to an attic at the very top of the house, as the other rooms arc all occupied by patients. You must not be alarmed if you hear stranp sounds on your way up. We have a very fractious woman in the house. She imagines my brother murdered her husband, and calls him dreadful names at the top of her voice." "Oh, I shall be thinking too much of Sydney to fear anything else," replied Veronica. "If lie were taken from me now, I should die." As the carriage stopped, the door of the house opened simultaneously. Dr. Bonsor stood upon the threshold with a welcoming smile. "lie is better, Miss Tempest. But I intend to keep him a little longer in a darkened room till the effects of the shock wear off. If you really wish to see him, you must enter very noiselessly." "Indeed, I will do anything you tell site, said Veronica, "but I must—l must see Sydney." She followed the doctor cautiously up the stairs. The atmosphere of the house seemed stifling her with its gloom. On the first landing she paused instinctively, for the sound of a desperate struggle caught her ear. Dr. Bonsor paused also and turned a trifle paler than before. Words of terror reached their ears : "Help I Help! She has escaped! Ths knife--"

A door on their left burst open and an immenso woman, with flaming red hair streaming over her shoulders and wild, mad eyes, rushed out upon them.

She passed Veronica and sprang at Dr. Bonsor's throat, with something bright and shining in her hand.

"Murderer!" she yelled. "Die! Die! Die!" A stream of blood, an awful groan, and Dr. Donsor fell to the ground with the horrible figure bending over hini,tadnainistering stab upon stab. Sick with horror Veronica fled down the stairs, conscious that the man Icho once asked her hand in marriage had now been brutally killed before her eyes. Her one idea was to reach the street and call for aid. Sydney's life might be in danger— her Sydney. As he dashed out she ran against someone on the doorstep, a tall figure who caught her in his arms and held her close to his heart. "Sydney!" she gasped. "Sydney, how can it be you ?."

"I had a presentiment something was wrong. I eft my friend and drove back to London. I 'ancied I saw you in a brougham with Nurse Dora, but you followed on the chance. What have they done to passed ci quickly 1 could not be sure. I 'f ou, my precious, my own ?" Veronica could not answer. She only looked up Into his eyes, realised he was safe, and dared not think of the rtst. "Take me away!" she pleaded. "Dr. Donsor has been murdered! For Heaven's sake take me Away from this terrible, terrible lionse--" He carried her half-fainting down the steps and lifted her into a cab. "You aro safe, my darling, qvite safe," he whispered. "We are going home.' LSo she lay with his arm around her without speaking, without thinking, weak and trembling still, but conscious of security.

• NATIONAL INSURANCE ACT. With reference to applications for appointments as auditors and inspectors under the National Insurance Act we are desired to state that none can be considered unless il, is made upon the official form supplied for the purpose. Such forms must be applied for by letter only (enclosing foolscap addressed envelope for reply) in the case of auditorships to "The Secretary to the Tre3sury, 55, Whitehall, S.W." and in the case of Inspectorships to " The Secretary to the Insurance Commission, at the same address.

Fearnhead.

RISLEY INDEPENDENT METHODIST CHI:RCM—Sum:lay school anniversiry sermons was preached in the above place on Sunday last by Mr. E Wynne Jones of Warring- ton, who spoke from the words "For His Sake " and in the evening from the text" Other men laboured, ye are entered into their labours," pointing out the pro- gressiveness of Christianity. In the morning the choir rendered the anthem " The march of life" and evening the anthems " Praise the King of heaven " and "Jesus is our Shepherd," in a creditable manner, the children singing very sweetly. Mrs Sanders, of Warrington, sang the solo " Nearer my God to Thee " with much feeling and fine expression. For the afternoon a musical service was held. The anthems 'What are these " and " Suffer little children " were rendered. Mrs. Sander's solo," For the children's sake" was very finely sung. Master James Bate.of Warrington,created a capital impression with the solo " Jesus once a little child." There were good congregations at all the services and the offerings amounted to the sum of £lO 2s

ST. HELENS.

ST. HELENS PAGEANT. 10,000 ROMAN CATHOLICS TAKE PART IN PROCESSIOM.

The annual demonstration of the Roman Catholic guilds, confraternities and schools of St. Helens was held on Monday, and was remarkably successful, being favoured by the most delightful weather. SJMO ten thousand persons participated in grand procession which was one of the most charming ever seen in the town. Before the procession each section from the nine missions of the town attended Mass at their own church and then marched to the Town Hall, where they were marshalled in processional orrler by their chief marshal,the Rev. Father Hearne. The Mayor (Sir Joseph Beecham) and the president of the Broughton Roman Catholic Charitable Society were on the Town Hall steps in front of which the children massed and sang the hymn Faith of Our Fathers" and the National Anthem, and then the procession started, occupying over an hour in passing the Town Hall, the Mayor remaining an interested spectator all the time. There were numerous bands, and each section carried several handsome banners, which in each instance were surronnded with groups of gaily attired children arranged in picturesque fashion, who were heartily cheered by the thousands of spectators who lined the route. Each church had its own special feature, and, considering that the people have only just emerged from a strike that has had a most serious effect on the trade of the town, they made a brave display, the effect, in many instances, being particularly striking, as one saw long lines of children dressed in unison, carrying flowers, daisy chains or gaily coloured streamers.

BOATING FATALITY AT ST. HELENS. The enjoyment of Whit Monday was sadly marred at St. Helens by a boating fatality in the lake at Taylor Park, where the public boating has proved very popular. Shortly before three o'clock, John Fulham, a young colliery banksman, of Central-terrace, Haydock, was boating with a companion named Charles James, also of Ilaydock. Fulbam was rowing, and, as they were passing the fishing stage on the deep side of the like, they decided to change seats. While this was being attempted the boat as upset, and both men were thrown into the water, which is about seven feet deep. James luckily came up on the side nearest the stage, and managedito clamber up, but Fulham sank, and about twenty minutes elapsed before his body was recovered. Constable Latham and boatman Crooks tried in vain to revive him, end after being seen by Dr. Challenor, the body was removed to the mortuary. ST. HELENS WEDDING.

The weddine was celebrated on Tuesday, at the church of St Dolls! of Canterbury, Windleshaw, St. Helens, of Miss Cisme Collins, second daughter of Councillor J A Collins, 3.1'.. of Hawthodene, Denton's Green, and Dr Neil Moxon, eldest son of Mr H MOXOD, of %Vest Park. The bridegroom is surgeon of the 11,,M,5. Empress of Ireland, C.P.R. Line, and the happy couple intend shortly to settle in Canada. Owing to the illness of Mr Moxon, sen., the wedding was of a quiet description, • the guests being solely members of the two families. The ceremony vras performed by the Rev Dr Bennett, and the wedding breakfast was after- wards served at the bride's borne. Mr IV Collins acted as hest man. The bridesmaids were Miss Iklabel Moxon and Miss May Collins.

JUMPED TO DEATH.

Catherine Knapper, aged forty-nine a married woman living at 21, Morris-street, Sutton, St. Helens, threw herself into a reservoir near Watery-lane, about six o'clock on Tuesday evening, and was drowned. The woman was seen by two men to be standing by the side of the water, and they raw her jump in, and immediately ran to the place, where they succeeded in recovering the body. Artificial restoration was tried for some time, but without avail, and the body was removed to the mortuary. add St Helena boating fatality Mr. S. Brighouse, held the inquest on Tuesday touch- ing the death of John Fulham, whose age was stated to be.forty-si.x:

After 'evidence of the sad occurrence had been given The Chief Constable stated that he warned John Crooks, tLe boat proprietor, that something of this sort would happm unless he had a policeman to assist on holidays so that no one under the influence of drink should get into the boats. Inspector Jackson said he warned the boat proprietor several times last season that he ought to have a policeman to assist him. The jury returned a verdict of " Accidental death." and asked the cororer to censure the boat proprietor.

FREE TRADE TRIUMPHANT.

===== 0002601_19120601_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
SHIPBUILDING AND SHIPPING.

By C. E. Parker.

Over a period of six years the world's out- put from ship-buildinglyards of seagoing vessels and the division between the United Kingdom and the rest of the world are as follows :

_ United o.her Kingdom Countries The World Year Tons Tons Tons 1906 1,828,343 1,091,420 2,919,763 1907 1,667,890 1,170,193 2,778,088 1908 929,669 903,617 1,833,286 1909 991,066 610,991 1,602,057 1910 1,143,169 814,684 1,957,853 1911 1,803,844 846,296 2,650,140

The outstanding fact of this table is the wonderful predominance of English ship- building. Of the total mercantile output of the world no less than 68 per cent. comes from English yards. In 1903 our pro- portion was 63Z. We are thus not only beat- ing every other individual country, but are increasing our lead against the whole world. Look at this fact any way you like and it is very remarkable. In cricket at the end of the season the premier county plays the rest of England. But in the case of ship- building it is as if the premier county played not a selected eleven, but all the other elevens combined. The world's shipbuilding apart from our own appears to be on the decline, or at best stationary, and it will probably remain so as long as foreign countries shackle this industry with pro- tective tariffs. No Protectionist country can ever compete with England in shipbuilding as long as these countries adhere to Protection.

===== 0002601_19120601_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
GERMANY-

Germany alone of the Protective countries shows any advance, and this is but slow. It would be difficult to imagine what Tariff Reformers would do without Germany. But to ensure advancement in this industry

I under the American flag. If it is worth ' while there are various ways of doing it. One is to give us absolute Free Trade." America has too tightly bound herself by the shackles of Protection to entertain any hope that she can attain her freedom by anything short of an industrial revolution. Hence this great country with its vast wealth in natural resources has to a large extent to depend on Free Trade England to build her ships and carry her products.

TBE TRANSPORT WORKERS' STRIKE.

Over 100,G00 men are now idle, and nearly the whole of the Port cf London trade is held up. Both masters and men say they are determined to fight the battle to a finish. To maintain the food supply of London, police escorts guard the vans on their way from the docks to the markets. Police reserves have been called up, and a large force will be available wherever required, for the maintenar.ce cf order rad the unchecked deliveiy of food, coal, petrol, and other commodities.

Germany has had to take off her protective tariffs in the hopes of creating a shipbuilding industry and carrying trade. But her out- put of merchant vessels in 1911 was only about i 265,000 tons against our own of the same of 1,803,000 tons. In Germany all materials for building, fitting, or repairing seagoing ships, not river steamers, except kitchen and cabin fittings, may be imported free. Hence Germany's shipbuilding has made considerable advance as compared with other Protectionist countries, although the advance compared with our own is small. Tariff Reformers try to deflect the inference from this fact by stating, so damaging to Protection, what is quite true, that the amount of foreign articles used in the build- ing of German ships is comparatively small, that the great bulk of the materials that go to the make up of German built ships is of home production, and hence they contend that the free import regulations make little differ- ence to German shipbuilding. But they over- look what is really the main factor, German steel makers and makers of other materials that enter into ship construction, instead of holding up these products to the usual figure they demand from their ordinary home buyers, accept prices from the shipbuilders which are reduced to the level of the world's price. They make a virtue of a necessity. Hence the shipbuilders in Germany get the advantage of the Free Trade regulations, not so much by the buying of foreign materials, but by the Free Trade provision obliging IGerman manufacturers to take the world's price. THE UNITED STATES.

In America the same, or similar provisions in respect to free imports of ship materials hold good, but like many of the provisions in the Dingley Tariff which\ appear to give the advantages of Free Trade, this is neutralised by restrictions which make the provisions valueless. The continuous rise in the out- put of the British building yards and the steady decline of American shipping is one of the most striking vindications of our Free Trade policy. We enjoy no advantage which America does not share. In the matter of raw material America has cheap coal and steel, an ample sea board and an ingenious people. There was a time when the tonnage of American shipping almost equalled our own. Like ourselves they had developed the technique of shipbuilding. This is often put forward as the reason for our pre-eminence in this industry, but before America adopted Protection her output of ships nearly equalled our own. It is often said that the American Civil War gave a set-back to the industry from which it has never recovered. But we too have had our wars and have recovered. The true explanation is to be found in the fact that following the Civil War, America headed for the backwaters of Protection while we steered for the open and have been carried forward by the favouring winds of Free Trade. The registered oversea tonnage of the United States in 1860 was over half our own; in 1911 it was only about one-tenth. Protection strangled an industry in which with the help of Free Trade, cheap coal and iron, America might have, ere this , led the world. The United States Commission on Merchant Shipping in 1904 'brought these facts to the front. Admiral Bowles in giving evidence remarked: "Somebody had to decide whether it is worth while to have a merchant marine

FRANCE.

In regard to France. This country is no doubt handicapped by want of coal and iron, but like America she has added to this handi- cap a heavier one in the Protective system. France has attempted to counterbalance these disadvantages by a wasteful system of giving bounties to shipbuilders on freights earned, and distances sailed. These have proved not only to be a serious drain upon the public funds, but have altogether failed to build up either a shipbuilding industry or a carrying trade.

===== 0002601_19120601_art0130.txt | included=true | included =====
ITALY.

In Italy as in France both shipbuilding and shipping are kept alive, and only kept alive, by the bounty system. It is stated that the bounties already paid amount to 50 per cent. more than the total value of the present mercantile fleet of Italy. And yet in spite of these bounties wrung from the poor Italians by a host of protective taxes, Italian shipbuilding is on the decline. Many of these protectionist countries are recognizing that they are unable to build ships in competition with the United King- dom and have adopted the sensible plan of buying ships from us, mqstly our old ones that, for our purposes, are out of date. We have thus got a good price for our old sailing and for the second-rate steamers. In this way we sell to foreign countries annually about 250,000 tons of shipping and the gap is filled by newer, larger, and more effective steamers. About one-third of the shipping added annually to the German register is composed of vessels bought from England. It was a result to be expected from our Free Trade policy that as we got clear of the morass and jungle of protective duties, Colonial preference, shipping bounties, and navigation restrictions there should natur- ally fall into our hands the great interna- tional trades which employ so many of our people, and which from their nature are incapable of being effectually protected. Shipping is one of these trades. Insur- ance, banking, brokerage, and our merchant trade are others. To-day it is not with any individual country we compare our merchant marine, but with that of all other countries together, and in this comparison we find although the rest of the world can in the aggregate show a slight excess of tonnage, yet in quality and efficiency, the balance is greatly in our favour as against the world combination. Our total foreign trade is not one-sixth that of the whole world, but we carry in our ships not one-sixth but one-half of the world's trade, and we build 68 per cent of the world's ships. Have we not a right to claim that Free Trade is triumphant?

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

RUNCORN'S WHITSUNTIDE PROCESSION. Whit Monday was especially notable in the history of Runcorn, for after forty-three years of separate and distinct Sunday school processions, the Church of England, Roman Catholics and Nonconformists going their own way—all the denominations joined hands for the first time, and united in one gigantic procession under most favourable conditions. Over 5,000 scholars, attending the various Sunday schools in the town, accompanied by seven bands made up one procession, which toured the principal bands, of the town and then marched on to the park. Arrange- ments were made by each school to provide a substan- tial tea for its scholars. A record was created by Mr. Jas. Higgins, formerly of Runcorn and now of Hoylake who made a special journey, and walked with his old school (Brunswick ‘Vesleyan) for the sixty-fourth successive year. RUNCORN EDUCATION. RESIGNATION OF OLD OFFICIALS. The Runcorn Education Sub-Committee had before them on Thursday evening the report of a special county Committee appointeceto consider the question of local administration. The report negatived a proposal that the Runcorn Urban and Runcorn Rural education authorities should be amalgamated. It recommended that, in view of the onerous character of the work and the growing infirmity of the clerk to the committee (Mr J Wightburn), a new appointment Was necessary, and it was suggested that his resignation be accepted and an application for compensation supported and that a successor be advertised for at £lBO a year. The resignation of Mr Thomas Bowyer, for thirty- seven years chief school attendance officer, was also accepted, and it was intimated that his application for a pension would be supported. It was recommended that the work be performed by one officer in future. A special committee was appointed to consider the suggested reorganisation and report.

YOUNG "BOOKIE" FINED. WIDNES MAGISTRATES AND STREET BETTING.

At Widnes, on Moncl-aTa-37Juiri man named Thomas Dillon, Lugsdale-road, was charged with loitering in a public passage off Charlotte-street for the purpose of betting last Thursday. Sergeant Davidson said he saw several people hand- ing what appeared to be slips and money at noon on Thursday. _ Later in the day he got the assistance of another constable, and they again saw slips and money handed to the defendant. When he was taken into custody and searched £3 13s 4cl in money was found in his possession, and slips for that day's racing amount- ing to 1 16s. On the day's results, compared with the slips, Dillon would only have to pay out 2s 6d. Major Waring: That's very good business! The Sergeant: It is sir ! Mr. Aitken: He might have lost all. Major Waring : Possibly, but not probably (laughter). The defendant said that owing to ill-health he had been obliged to do something. He had saved a few pounds, and was trying to make a living in this way. Mr. Aitkin : You thought you would have a soft job ?—No, sir. I don't think it's a "snip." The bench fined the defendant £5 and costs, and added that they intended to stop this sort of street batting.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

EARLESTOWN RAILWAY TRAGEDY. HIS LIFE FOR HIS FRIEND.

[low an Earlestown man named William- Eaves lost his life in heroically attempting to save an old man named Thomas Potts was told at tbe inquest at Ear lestown on Tuesday. The two men were working at the London and North Western Railway waggon wore, and Potts got between a waggon and another that was approaching. Ems leaned over the buffers to sore his old comrade and was himself caught between the buffers and crushed to death. A verdict of " Accidental death " was returned, and the coroner and jury ()Oared the widow (who is left with nine young children) the alight consolation that her husband had died a hero in every sense of the word. The coroner promised to aid in obtaining benefit for the widow and children from a fund formed for the purpose. -------

. . NEW COUNCIL SCHOOL. £10145 TENDER ACCEPTED. The Earlestown Education Committee bas considered tenders from various firma (nine in all) for the erection of the new Council School in Paterson-street. It was resolved that the tender of Messrs D A Ablett and Sons, of Wigan be accepted. This tender amounted to the sum of £10,145, and it was the lowest tender by the considerable sum of £243, When the committee's decision has been confirmed by the County Committee, the work will be commenced immediately.

and Health. Sir,—Mr. Dukes in his letter of the 25th asks to be allowed to make his position clear. He has done so, anti shows it to be, we think, rather inconsistent. " Let us " says Mr. Dukes "take a view of the position, and suppose the worker to be compelled. to undergo military training. Now military training demands a stamina upon which to build. Those who know any thing of the conditions of labour, know that the high intensification of labour in the modern work- shop, renders those employed more fitted for a quiet holiday and rest at the seaside than for the physical exertion demanded for military training. This is more likely to oppress the worker than to build him up." As Mr. Dukes approaches the end cf his letter he seems to have forgotten what he had stated at the onset, and says " I believe in a citizen army, not as a means of liberating the standing army for the pur- poses named, but as a means of its abolition." How would Mr. Dukes propose to train this " citizen army" in which he believes? Where would he find that "stamina " which he considers so necessary ? Why advocate the abolition of the standing army while pleading that the workingman is too tired to drill? "Physical regeneration" says Mr. Dukes "is au economic problem." Yes, in part, but not absolutely. What about the thousands of well-to-do people in our great cities who spend a large proportion of their time in the pursuit of the gaieties of life, and -whose health suffers in consequence. Many of these would he better off physically if they had less money. In certain residential districts not far from Nar- rington less than 40 per cent of the houses have thtir bedroom windows open during the night. Do these people breath poisoned air because of bad economic conditions ? Certainly the economic problem is important, but only deals with part of the difficulty, and if solved will only be found to have furnished a factor--most valuable no doubt— for dealing with a

We agree that the working man has a right to a far greater share in the good things of life than he has hitherto enjoyed, and it must be admitted that important reforms are contemplated with this end in view. But such admissions only magnify the import- ance of the N.S.L. proposals, for what is the use of preparing elaborate schemes having for their object the well-being of the democracy unless at the same time preparation is made to give to them a reasonable guarantee of permanency?—Yours truly, G. DALE.

THE DUMP COFFIN.

ANOTHER TORY TRICK EXPOSED.

The Warrington Tariff Reformers' chickens are always coming home to roost in a most unpleasant and unfilial way. The German clocks have barely ceased ticking in the sensitive ears of Lady Greenall and her fellow promoters of the great Dump Exhibi- tion, when another ghost emerges from the past to affright their eyes. This time it is a coffin—the coffin. Free Traders and Tariff Reformers alike will remember the coffin which occupied such a place of honour at the Exhibition. It bore the legend "Free Trade died here December 3, 1910," which was intended for a piece of delicate Tory satire. Unfortunately for the anti-dumpers the coffin turns out to be the biggest joke of the whole exhibition and will need to be buried very deep before it can be forgotten. We are indebted to the "Daily News and Leader" for the full story of the exhumation and the shocking revelationsat the subsequent inquest Our contemporary writes as follows:

COFFIN TRICK. SIDELIGHT ON TARIFF REFORM METHODS. A funny story of how the Tariff Reform League worked a coffin trick is divert- ing Free Traders. Some genius associated with the league, after meditating like Harvey among the dusty commercial tombs in the league's dumper goods warehouse in Salford, con- cluded that it would be a deadly blow for Free Trade to include the British coffin- making industry among those whose feeble health demands a dose of Protection. No foreign coffins, it happens, are imported into this country. That, at any rate, is a fact, and the reason is that, apart from the absence of any demand, the freight on such bulky goods renders such a traffic out of the question. Still, the Tariff Reform League had to make it out somehow that the British coffin-making business was, so to speak, on its last trestles. What was done therefore was to order from Mr. Samuel Yates, a well- known coffin builder, whose factory is in Lower Broughton, Manchester, the most economical specimen that can be turned out. This is a coffin costing, unpolished, 18s.

_ The order was duly executed and the coffin xemoved to the Salford warehouse. From there it was afterwards taken with other examples of foreign competi- tion—more or less authentic—to War- rington. There it figured in a Tariff Reform League exhibition as an article " dumped " from Sweden. For the occasion the coffin wore an inscription telling how this sort of thing was bring- ing the British undertaker, as it were, with sorrow to the grave. When Mr. Yates learned from a mutual friend, a Tariff Reformer, who had seen the article on show, he was naturally annoyed, and he was much more annoyed to find that currency had been given to an allegation that his firm imported coffins from Sweden. Needless to say, the allegation was as unfounded and silly as the " information " given in the exhibition label A "Daily News and Leader" repre- sentative who made an inquiry at the Manchester offices of the Tariff Reform League was officially assured that the League had never had a coffin to its name, and that though the stock-in- trade ih.t the Salford dumped goods depot includes a selection of foreign tombstones, a coffin is not to be found within its doors. Mr. Yates, however, is in possession of the League's order. Seeing that he has "cut up rough" about it, the coffin has probably by this time been used to bury some of the worn-out fiscal litera- ture or to refresh the fires of tariff enthusiasm.

and strain tend to thrust into the backgrcund. All Thursday morning people were pouring into the village by train, brake and cycle and the kcal caterers viewed the invasion with delight. At two o'clock the old, viindirg streets, gay with-flags, were almost impassable, while the weather-beaten steps of the village Cross were also tightly packed with sightseers. The breakdown of the weather caused the procession to be sent on its way a little earlier than was intended, but those who saw its marshalling and departure were able to form a better idea of the beauty of the spectacle than others who had taken up positions further along the route, only to be confronted with a depressing array of umbrellas and closed carriages. About 350 children took part in the procession which included all the familiar features f such displays, as well as some new attractions. Considerable ingenuity was exhibited in the devising of five tableaux. The first showed a stately Britannia and loyal sub- jects; the second gave striking representations of "the seasons " ; the third concentrated attention success- ively upon the eharactelistics of " past, present and future" ; the fourth hail as its subj(ct some of the old nursery rhymes; while fisally came "The old weman in a shoe" with her numerous and troublesome family. Then appeared a host of characters from history and nursery lore. The Duke cf Wellington fraternized with his arch-enemy Napoleon; by a freak of circum- stance Lord Nelson and Sir Walter Raleigh became contemporaries, while that fearsome highway man Dick Turpin arerised no dread in the minds of Grace Darling and her father who follow. d him in the pageant. There were also the sainted "Maid of Orlearis," Robin Hood and his mei rymen in Lincoln green, Cinderella, Boy Blue and Bo Peep and a bevy of demeuie Quakeresses. Groups cf dancers in fancy costumes iicled further touches of variety, and there were the inevitable representatives of .varicus nationalities Queen Elizabeth arid Maiy Queen of Sects were dignified figures, but they were not the only regal visitants from the past, for the five King Georges were also in evidence. Preceded by beefeaters, armed with halberds, came the carriage in which rode the May Queen, with her maids cf honour and attendants. "Her Majesty" was Miss Rachael Odd ie, a bright, fair-haired girl—the daughter of the headmaster of the Parochial schouls—whose choice was generally acclaimed as highly popular. She comported herself with true dignity and acknowledged with smile and bow the plaudits of her subjects. "Her Majesty " wore a beautiful dress of white satin, with Brussels lace aleevEs, and a red train trimmed with ermine. The maids cf honour

tiny tots in picturesque Spanish costume ' gave a captivating tambourine dance. The St. Joseph's boys are accomplished in the Terpichorean art and the hornpipe, Irish jig and folk dances which they performed with such ease and neatness were among the m^st enjoyable features of the programme. Miss L Jackson, of Alt rincham, acquitted herself brilliantly in a couple of solo dances, full of grace and fascina- tion. The first was a delightful measure to the tune " In the shadows " and the second was a pleasing mazurka. Some orthe village children performed a quaint Swedish dance—a real national dance on the reheareig of which much care must have been expended and the old English dance,reminiscent of the " Merrie England " of yore, was a highly appreciated item. Finally there was a national dance, cleverly introducing the different sections of the United Kingdom, the whole concluding with a picturesque tableau. We cannot speak too highly of the work of Miss M. Field, of Ileatley, who had trained the children for the dances and thus contributed so much to the pleasure of the visitors. The set programme having been disposed of, dancing was indulged in until another deluge caused a dispersal of the throng. The festival was fortunate in its officers who had devoted much self-sacrificing effort to plan the arrangements on the most complete and businesslike lines. Some of the ladies are entitled to special mention, notably Mrs James D B Smith, the energetic bon. secretary of the ladies committee ; Mrs Swain, Mrs C Wright, Mrs E Rushton and Mrs E Moores, who so cordially backed up her work. The General Committee had an ideal chairman in Mr Jas. D B Smith, whose enthusiastic lieutenant was Mr A A Hudson. The duties of hon, treasurer were admirably undertaken by Mr II S Saville, and Mr A II Hudson. Mr James Webb, jun., was a tireless and efficient bon. secretary, and he had a capable assistant in Mr A B Edwards. The M.C.'s were Mr 'l' Kish and Mr F P Cooke. The ladies' committee was under the able presidency of Miss M. Field, and the members were :—Mrs C Lewis, Mrs Swain, Misses C Boond, E Hesketh, flazlehurst, M Kirk. E Moores, N Pearson, E Rushton and E Wright. , The gentlemen's c )mrnittfe consisted of the Illowing: Messrs F F Cowsill, F W Druce F Eat .n. We studied this as our energetic visitor got up and impatiently paced the sanctum floor. The book contained the result of observations taken by himself, during specified hours and different periods, giving the thinutes and seconds during which black smoke had been emitted by the offending chimneys. Every- one of these manufacturers specified had exceeded the limit of three consecutive minutes and also the total of five minutes to the half-hour, and exceeded it not by a few seconds, but by many minutes. After mastering the details we looked up to our impatient friend and asked him what he suggested we should do with these pages of figures? "Print them, print them" he replied, "and let the people of Warrington see what a hollow sham these inspections are, and what a mockery this part of the Health Com- mittee's work is."

" Yes" we replied, " but will we not be met by the reply that your standard of what is black smoke may be different from that of the Health Committee ? "

" Nonsense " he replied, "there can be no misunderstanding. When a thing is black it is black, you cannot make it into any other colour. If you think I do not know when smoke is black set a reporter to make obser- vations for the " ExanTiner."

" But surely there must be some mistake. The Health Committee's rule for black smoke is three minutes in the hour and they would never allow their own rule to be so persist- ently broken." "You are more simple than I thought an Editor to be. Do you not understand that the whole thing is the result of funk. The Health Committee have not courage to put in force their own regulations! Do you not see that in a small town like Warrington members of the Council and Health Com- mittee are so mixed up with the manu- facturing interests they are afraid to give offence, and therefore their own rules are allowed to exist only as dummies, and the health and cleanliness of all who live in the town suffer. As far as the smoke nuisance is concerned the Health Committee are simply jellyfish ! jellyfish ! " "We suppose you know what the members of the Council will say if urged to take more active means to make the manufacturers control their smoke ?"

" Yes, yes" he replied, "I know what you are getting at. You are going to tell me that the Town Councillors will give the threadbare excuse, and say they will not enforce their own regulations if the result of a more vigorous enforcement is likely to frighten the manufacturers away from the town. Now that is what you were going to say ?"

"Yes" !we replied, " you have touched the spot,"

"Well, a more rotten reason was never given. Nobody wants to enforce rules in an unreasonable way. This is always the excuse trotted out, it has been the excuse in every attempt to control manufacturers for the public good since the bringing in of the early Factory Regulations. There has never been a law brought forward, but the same empty excuse has been given; regulations for child labour, women labour, cleansing factories, ample air- space, sufficient lights, over-time for young persons, compensation for accidents, and all the rest—always the same excuse, always the same shelter for inaction. Manufacturers like all other people have to be made to realize that they exist not for their own convenience and profit alone, but also for the good of the community, and if they inflict injury on the community they must be restrained. Nobody asks for an unreasonable enforcement of the law about black smoke. All we ask, and have the right to demand, is that manufacturers conform to what is the outcome of the well-considered regulations and powers with which public authorities are entrusted. The weakness of the War- rington authorities arises from personal fear which prevents them doing what they know is for the public good." "But you will surely allow that we have got a good Health Committee, who are steadily improving the town ? " We found the Medical Officer's report and showed him that in 1910 over two thousand nuisances were dealt with by the committee. " Aye " he replied, "that's where the irony of the thing comes in. The Health Committee are energetic when they have only the poor and small fry to consider, but when it comes to a question of tackling a big manufacturer, or a powerful firm, they have no more pluck than a louse. (He was very breezy.') Over two thousand petty nuisances, but how many cases of smoke nuisances? Only ten, and these were let off with a caution,' and the reports put away in a pigeon hole. Just sufficient to make a show in the annual report. The manufacturers know the com- mittee does not mean business, put forward some excuse (which the committee is only too pleased to be satisfied with) and go on pouring out black smoke." "But you will allow that the Health Com- mittee have lately shown a commendable zeal in visiting the slum property during the Health Week?" "Yes," he replied, "I have nothing to say against that; it is meant kindly, but look at the absurdity of the thing. What more than anything makes dirty courts, dirty homes, and dirty people than the foul air which ' blocks out the sunlight, and day and night drops its soot and filth on everything ? Then the Members of the Health Committee, who weakly allow, and tacitly encourage this air pollution, come to the chief sufferers and would instruct them about keeping their surroundings clean, opening their windows for the soot to get in, &c. Would not

===== 0002601_19120601_art0147.txt | included=true | included =====

SMOKE.

HOW WE MET THE WHIRLWIND.

We were busy correcting copy when a visitor was ushered in. He was big and breezy, He stood over us and without pre- liminaries plumped out the question "What is the ' Examiner ' going to do about the abominable smoke nuisance ? " We were somewhat taken aback,and asked if he would kindly sit down and explain explicitly what he meant. "It should require no explanation. I always thought a newspaper worth its salt had eyes that see all over the town and everything that goes on in it. Is it not obvious to you that Warrii gton for its size is one of the smokiest towns in the country, and the authorities are doing nothing to improve it ?" _

--"But," we replied, "we have a capable Town Council, a worthy Mayor, and an efficient Health Committee."

"Capable Town Council ! ! Jellyfish—jelly- fish, nothing but a shoal of jellyfish, not a bit of backbone in the lot!"

" Well but "we replied " there is a Smoke Inspector who every year makes a number of' observations ' and we suppose action is taken upon the result of these by the Health Committee."

"Goodallsif SPECIALISTS IN OUTSIDE SUN BLINDS ESTIMATES FREE. KING ST. MANCHESTER.

Pure Indian Tea commands the markets of the world. India exports in a year 249,000,000 lbs. of well manufactured tea of which the United Kingdom consumes 169,000,000 lbs. So keen is the desire for the delicious teas from India that the de- mand has out-run the supply, and the dealer is tempted to keep down the price of his blends by substituting inferior teas from fore-gn countries for the genuine Indian product. These substitutes have neither the richness nor the purity of Indian tea. It is therefore desirable for the purchaser to obtain a guarantee that the tea sold her is genuine Indian. Considerations of economy, of health, and of comfort, unite in recommend- ing this course.
